[HEADING level=3 role=subsection]
Conditions for floating

[PARAGRAPH]
For an object to float, the upthrust exerted by the surrounding fluid must be greater than the objects weight. Upthrust depends on the volume of the submerged part of the object and the density of the surrounding fluid. Thus, when an object is placed in a fluid, the greater the submerged volume of the object, the greater the upthrust.

[PARAGRAPH]
A ship is normally built with a large hollow space. Typically, the ship has a large volume which allows it to displace a large volume of water, resulting into a large upthrust equalising the weight of the ship. If the hollow space is filled with air, the average density of the ship material and air is less than the density of water and so, the ship floats. Therefore, the following conditions must be satisfied for an object to float in water:

[END_PAGE]

[PDF_PAGE 102]
[PRINTED_PAGE 96]

[RUNNING_HEADER]
Physics for Secondary Schools

[HEADING level=3 role=subsection]
Concept of upthrust

[TASK label="Task 5.3"]
Hold a stone in one hand and sense its weight. Tie the same stone using a string and submerge it in water. Hold the string and try to pull it. What do you feel about its weight?

[PARAGRAPH]
Consider a cork being held at the bottom of a beaker that contains a liquid, as shown in Figure 5.3 (a). When the cork is released, it immediately rises to the surface of the liquid, as shown in Figure 5.3(b).

[CAPTION label="Figure 5.3"]
Figure 5.3: Upthrust in water

[PARAGRAPH]
The rising of the cork shows that, while inside the liquid, an upward force acts on it. Since this force is greater than the weight of the cork, it is pushed up to the surface of the liquid. An upward force acting on an object that is totally or partially immersed in a fluid is called an upthrust or buoyant force. Upthrust enables an object to float or feel lighter in a fluid.

[PARAGRAPH]
When one is swims in water, his or her weight is balanced by the upthrust acting on the body. Water vessels, like ships and boats, float and sail on water due to this force, otherwise, they would sink. The tendency or ability of an object to float on a fluid suspended on it is called buoyancy and the upward force exerted by a fluid on that object is called upthrust (buoyant force). Buoyancy is the force that determines whether an object will sink, float, or remain suspended in a fluid. This concepts was best described by Archimedes, who famously shouted "Eureka!" when he discovered the phenomenon.

[PARAGRAPH]
In addition, an object is said to experience neutral buoyancy when its average density is equal to the density of the surrounding fluid. In this state, the buoyant force is precisely equal to the gravitational force acting on the object, resulting in a net force of zero. As a consequence, the object remains suspended at a specific depth in the fluid without any vertical acceleration. Neutral buoyancy is particularly important in various fields, including:

[PARAGRAPH]
Scuba diving; divers use buoyancy control devices (BCDs) to achieve neutral buoyancy, allowing them to hover at a desired depth without sinking or floating to the surface.

[PARAGRAPH]
Underwater research and engineering; neutral buoyancy is employed in underwater laboratories and simulators to conduct experiments and train astronauts for spacewalks (extravehicular activities or EVAs) as the microgravity conditions in space are similar to those of neutral buoyancy.

[PARAGRAPH]
Marine biology; researchers and divers use neutral buoyancy to study marine life without disturbing the natural environment.

[END_PAGE]

[PDF_PAGE 103]
[PRINTED_PAGE 97]

[RUNNING_HEADER]
Sinking and floating

[HEADING level=3 role=subsection]
Apparent weight of an object

[PARAGRAPH]
If one attempts to lift an object in a swimming pool and then, attempts to lift the same object outside of the pool, it feels much lighter in the pool than when outside. This is the effect of upthrust. When a body is totally or partially immersed in a fluid, it experiences an upthrust exerted by the fluid. The upthrust is equal to the weight of the fluid displaced by the immersed object. Because of upthrust, an object that is submerged in the fluid appears to weigh less than its actual weight in air. The weight of the submerged object is known as apparent weight, while its weight in air is referred to as the actual weight. The difference between the weight and the apparent weight of an object is referred to as the apparent loss in weight.

[PARAGRAPH]
Therefore,

[EQUATION]
\text{Apparent loss in weight} = \text{Actual weight} - \text{Apparent weight of body in fluid}
[EQUATION]
= \text{Weight of the displaced fluid}
[EQUATION]
= \text{Upthrust}
[PARAGRAPH]
Thus, Upthrust = Apparent loss in weight.

[BEGIN ACTIVITY label="Activity 5.2"]
[HEADING level=3 role=other]
Activity 5.2

[OBJECTIVES]
Aim: To determine the apparent loss in weight of objects immersed in water.

[PARAGRAPH]
Materials: Measuring cylinder, spring balance, solid objects such as stones, water, string

[INSTRUCTION]
Procedure

[LIST style=numbered]
1. Tie the stone using a string and measure its weight in air using a spring balance as shown in Figure 5.4. Record this weight as \(w_1\).
2. Fill a measuring cylinder with water to about 30 ml of its volume.
3. Lower the stone suspended at the end of the spring balance into the water. It should be partially immersed. Note the reading on the spring balance as \(w_2\) and the level of liquid in the measuring cylinder.
4. Lower the stone such that it is totally immersed in the water, as shown in Figure 5.4 and record this weight as \(w_3\).
5. Remove the stone, dry and re-weigh it in air. Record this weight as \(w_4\).
6. Calculate the apparent loss in weight of the stone and record it as \(W_L\).
7. Repeat steps 1 to 6 for other objects, one at a time.

[END ACTIVITY]

[END_PAGE]

[PDF_PAGE 104]
[PRINTED_PAGE 98]

[RUNNING_HEADER]
Physics for Secondary Schools

[CAPTION label="Figure 5.4"]
Figure 5.4: Measuring weight in air and water using spring balance

[HEADING level=4 role=other]
Questions

[QUESTION label="(a)"]
What do you notice about the values \(w_1\), \(w_2\), \(w_3\), \(w_4\) and \(W_L\)?

[QUESTION label="(b)"]
What is your conclusion about the weight of the stone?

[PARAGRAPH]
In Activity 5.2, you have learnt that, the weight of an object in air (\(w_1\)) is larger than its weight when it is submerged in water (\(w_2\)). That is, there is an apparent loss in weight (\(w_L\)) which is the difference between the weight in air and the weight in liquid. Thus,

[EQUATION]
\text{Apparent loss in weight} = \text{weight in air} - \text{weight in water}
[EQUATION]
w_L = w_1 - w_2 \quad \text{or} \quad w_L = w_1 - w_3
[PARAGRAPH]
Note that the apparent loss in weight is equal to the upthrust exerted on the object by water.

Sinking and floating

[HEADING level=2 role=section]
Archimedes' Principle

[PARAGRAPH]
If an object is immersed in water, it displaces some of the water. The relationship between the upthrust acting on a body and the weight of the displaced fluid, when the body is partially or totally immersed in the fluid was discovered by a Greek scientist known as Archimedes. This relationship is explained by Archimedes' principle, which is referred to as the law of buoyancy.

[BEGIN NOTE kind=definition]
Archimedes' principle states that, "Any object partially or totally immersed in a fluid experiences an upthrust equal to the weight of the fluid displaced by the object".

[PARAGRAPH]
It is important to note that the weight of an object acts downwards while the upthrust exerted by the displaced fluid acts upwards.

[HEADING level=3 role=subsection]
Verification of Archimedes' principle

[PARAGRAPH]
To verify the Archimedes principle, perform the following activity.

[BEGIN ACTIVITY label="Activity 5.3"]
[HEADING level=3 role=other]
Activity 5.3

[OBJECTIVES]
Aim: To verify the Archimedes' Principle.

[PARAGRAPH]
Materials: Beaker, eureka can, spring balance, measuring cylinder, stone, water and digital balance.

[CAPTION label="Figure 5.5"]
Figure 5.5: Experimental setup to verify Archimedes' principle

[INSTRUCTION]
Procedure

[LIST style=numbered]
1. Measure the weight of the stone in the air using a spring balance and record its weight.
2. Using a digital balance, measure the mass of a dry empty measuring cylinder, and record its mass.
3. Pour water into a eureka can up to its spout level.
4. Place the measuring cylinder under the spout of the eureka can.
5. Weigh the stone when it is totally immersed in water and record its weight. Wait until all the displaced water overflows into the measuring cylinder.
6. Weigh the measuring cylinder containing the displaced water. Record its weight.

Questions

[QUESTION label="(b)"]

[QUESTION label="(d)"]
How does the upthrust relate to the weight of the displaced water?

[QUESTION label="(e)"]

[END ACTIVITY]

[END_PAGE]

[PDF_PAGE 106]
[PRINTED_PAGE 100]

[RUNNING_HEADER]
Physics for Secondary Schools

[PARAGRAPH]
In Activity 5.3, you observed that upthrust is equal to the weight of the displaced fluid. This observation verifies Archimedes' principle, which applies to objects of all densities. It is important to note that upthrust depends on the weight of the fluid displaced.

[BEGIN EXAMPLE label="Example 5.1"]
[EXAMPLE]
In an experiment to study the behaviour of the weight of an object a student finds that the weight of the object is 4.9 N in air and its weight when completely submerged in water is reduced to 3.1 N. Calculate the upthrust.

[SOLUTION]
Weight of the object in air = 4.9 N
weight of the object in water = 3.1 N

\(\text{upthrust} = \text{weight of an object in air} - \text{weight of an object in water}\)

\(= 4.9\,\mathrm{N} - 3.1\,\mathrm{N}\)

\(= 1.8\,\mathrm{N}\)

Therefore, upthrust acting on the object is 1.8 N.
[END EXAMPLE]

[BEGIN EXAMPLE label="Example 5.2"]
[EXAMPLE]
A body immersed in water displaces 1.1 N. If its weight while in water was 3.3 N, find its weight in air.

[SOLUTION]
Weight of the displaced water = 1.1 N
Weight of the body in water = 3.3 N

\(\text{Upthrust} = 1.1\,\mathrm{N}\)

\(\text{Weight of the body in air} = \text{Apparent loss in weight} + \text{Apparent weight}\)

\(= 3.3\,\mathrm{N} + 1.1\,\mathrm{N} = 4.4\,\mathrm{N}\)

Therefore, weight of the body in air is 4.4 N.
[END EXAMPLE]

[END_PAGE]

[PDF_PAGE 107]
[PRINTED_PAGE 101]

[RUNNING_HEADER]
Sinking and floating

[HEADING level=3 role=subsection]
Factors affecting upthrust

[PARAGRAPH]
According to Archimedes' principle, an object submerged in a fluid experiences an upthrust that is equal to the weight of the fluid displaced. That is,

[EQUATION]
\text{Upthrust } (U) = \text{Weight of displaced fluid } (w_f)
[PARAGRAPH]
But,
Weight of the fluid displaced \((w_f) = \text{mass } (m_f) \times \text{acceleration due to gravity } (g)\)
Mass of the fluid \((m_f) = \text{density of the fluid } (\rho_f) \times \text{volume of the displaced fluid } (V_f)\)

[EQUATION]
m_f = \rho_f V_f
[PARAGRAPH]
In Chapter Four, you learned that the volume of the displaced fluid is equivalent to the volume of the immersed object. That is,
Volume of the fluid displaced \((V_f) = \text{volume of object } (V_o)\)

[EQUATION]
U = \rho_f V_o g
[PARAGRAPH]
Hence, upthrust is affected by:
(i) the volume of the immersed object;
(ii) the density of the fluid in which the object is immersed; and
(iii) the acceleration due to gravity.

[BEGIN EXAMPLE label="Example 5.3"]
[EXAMPLE]
Calculate the resulting buoyant force, if a steel ball of radius 6 cm is immersed in water. Assume density of water is \(1000\,\mathrm{kg\,m^{-3}}\) and acceleration due to gravity is \(10\,\mathrm{N\,kg^{-1}}\).

[SOLUTION]
Given data:
Radius of steel ball \(= 6\,\mathrm{cm} = 0.06\,\mathrm{m}\)
Density of water, \(\rho = 1000\,\mathrm{kg\,m^{-3}}\)
Acceleration due to gravity, \(g = 10\,\mathrm{N\,kg^{-1}}\)

Volume of steel ball, \(V = \frac{4}{3}\pi r^3 = \frac{4}{3}\pi(0.06)^3 = 9.04 \times 10^{-4}\,\mathrm{m^3}\)

\(U_B = \rho V g = 1000\,\mathrm{kg\,m^{-3}} \times 9.04 \times 10^{-4}\,\mathrm{m^3} \times 10\,\mathrm{N\,kg^{-1}} = 9.04\,\mathrm{N}\)

The magnitude of upthrust is 9.04 N.
[END EXAMPLE]

[END_PAGE]

[PDF_PAGE 108]
[PRINTED_PAGE 102]

[RUNNING_HEADER]
Physics for Secondary Schools

[BEGIN EXAMPLE label="Example 5.4"]
[EXAMPLE]
A man whose weight is 690 N contains \(5.3 \times 10^{-3}\,\mathrm{m^3}\) of blood. Calculate the weight of the blood if its density is \(1060\,\mathrm{kg\,m^{-3}}\).

[SOLUTION]
Given data:
Volume of blood \((V) = 5.3 \times 10^{-3}\,\mathrm{m^3}\)
Density of blood \((\rho) = 1060\,\mathrm{kg\,m^{-3}}\)

Recall, \(w = mg = \rho V g\)
\(w = 1060\,\mathrm{kg\,m^{-3}} \times 5.3 \times 10^{-3}\,\mathrm{m^3} \times 10\,\mathrm{N\,kg^{-1}} = 56.18\,\mathrm{N}\)

Therefore the weight of the blood is 56.18 N.
[END EXAMPLE]

[HEADING level=3 role=subsection]
Determination of relative density using the Archimedes' principle

[PARAGRAPH]
In Chapter Four, you learnt the concept of relative density (R.D) of a substance. The relative density of a substance was expressed as:

[EQUATION]
\text{R.D} = \frac{\text{density of a substance}}{\text{density of water}}
[PARAGRAPH]
In this chapter, the concept of relative density will be discussed using Archimedes principle. According to Archimedes' principle, the upthrust acting on the body immersed in water is equal to the weight of water displaced.

[EQUATION]
\text{R.D} = \frac{\text{mass of substance}}{\text{mass of equal volume of water}} = \frac{\text{weight of substance in air}}{\text{apparent loss in weight of water}} = \frac{\text{weight of substance in air}}{\text{upthrust}}
[END_PAGE]

[PDF_PAGE 110]
[PRINTED_PAGE 104]

[RUNNING_HEADER]
Physics for Secondary Schools

[PARAGRAPH]

[QUESTION label="(d)"]

[END ACTIVITY]

[PARAGRAPH]
Relative density of the stone can be calculated from the obtained results. For example, if the stone weighs 25 N in air and 17 N in water, then:

[EQUATION]
\text{R.D} = \frac{25\,\mathrm{N}}{(25-17)\,\mathrm{N}} = \frac{25\,\mathrm{N}}{8\,\mathrm{N}} = 3.125

[BEGIN EXAMPLE label="Example 5.5"]
[EXAMPLE]
In an experiment to determine the relative density of a liquid, a solid Q weighed as follows: Weight in air \(w_0 = 8.6\,\mathrm{N}\), Weight in water \(w_1 = 6.0\,\mathrm{N}\), Weight in liquid \(w_2 = 5.4\,\mathrm{N}\). Calculate the relative density of the liquid.

[SOLUTION]
\(\text{Upthrust in liquid} = 8.6\,\mathrm{N} - 5.4\,\mathrm{N} = 3.2\,\mathrm{N}\)
\(\text{Upthrust in water} = 8.6\,\mathrm{N} - 6.0\,\mathrm{N} = 2.6\,\mathrm{N}\)

\(\text{Relative density of liquid} = \frac{\text{Upthrust in liquid}}{\text{Upthrust in water}} = \frac{3.2\,\mathrm{N}}{2.6\,\mathrm{N}} = 1.2\)
[END EXAMPLE]

[END_PAGE]

[PDF_PAGE 113]
[PRINTED_PAGE 107]

[RUNNING_HEADER]

[RUNNING_HEADER]
Physics for Secondary Schools

[HEADING level=2 role=section]
Law of floatation

[BEGIN NOTE kind=definition]
The law of floatation states that: "A floating body displaces its own weight of the fluid in which it floats".

[BEGIN ACTIVITY label="Activity 5.7"]
[HEADING level=3 role=other]
Activity 5.7

[OBJECTIVES]
Aim: To investigate the relationship between upthrust and floatation.

[PARAGRAPH]
Material: Piece of wood, transparent beaker, water, spring balance, digital balance, weights

[CAPTION label="Figure 5.8"]
Figure 5.8: Investigating upthrust and floatation

[INSTRUCTION]
Procedure

[LIST style=numbered]
1. Suspend a piece of wood from a hook of a spring balance shown in Figure 5.8 (a). Record its weight.
2. Weigh a dry and empty beaker shown in Figure 5.8 (b).
3. Fill the transparent beaker with water to about 3/4 of its height and weight it as shown in Figure 5.8 (c).
4. Place the wood on the surface of the water and record its apparent weight.
5. Measure and record the water level when the wood is placed on the surface of the water.
6. Place a small weight on the floating wood.
7. Measure the water level and the depth of submersion of the wood after adding each weight.
8. Continue adding weights gradually, recording the water displacement and submersion depth each time, until the object begins to sink.

[HEADING level=4 role=other]
Questions

[QUESTION label="(a)"]
How the upthrust is related to the ability of the object to float?

[QUESTION label="(b)"]
Describe the conditions under which the object floats or sinks, based on the balance between the weight of the object and the upthrust.

[END ACTIVITY]

[END_PAGE]

[PDF_PAGE 115]
[PRINTED_PAGE 109]

[RUNNING_HEADER]
Sinking and floating

[HEADING level=3 role=subsection]
Relationship between upthrust and floatation

[PARAGRAPH]
Upthrust depends on the density of the fluid. If the density of an object is less than that of the surrounding fluid, the object will float. If the density of the object is larger than the density of the fluid, the object will sink. This is the reason why an object seems lighter, for example, in salt water than in fresh water and appears to weigh less in water than in air. A ship floats more easily in the sea, and also it is easier to swim in the ocean than in the lake because sea water is denser than lake water.

[CAPTION label="Figure 5.9"]
Figure 5.9: Ship vessel floating on a sea

[PARAGRAPH]
For an object to float on a fluid the upthrust exerted on it by the fluid must be equal to the weight of the object. That is, \(U = w\). Since, upthrust is equal to the weight of the displaced fluid, then,

[EQUATION]
\text{Weight of the displaced liquid} = \text{Apparent loss in weight of the object}
[PARAGRAPH]
Then, for an object to float, its apparent weight must be zero.

[END_PAGE]

[PDF_PAGE 116]
[PRINTED_PAGE 110]

[RUNNING_HEADER]
Physics for Secondary Schools

[HEADING level=3 role=subsection]
Relationship between volume of submerged part of the floating body and the density of fluid

[PARAGRAPH]
Consider a cork submerged in water of a particular volume and density. The two forces acting on a submerged body are upthrust U, and the weight of a floating body, \(W_B\). When an equilibrium is reached, these two forces balance. Hence,

[EQUATION]
\text{The upthrust in a liquid} = \text{weight of the liquid displaced}
[CAPTION label="Figure 5.10"]
Figure 5.10: A cork submerged in water

[PARAGRAPH]
Consider a body of volume \(V_1\) and density \(\rho_1\). Volume of submerged part is \(V_2\). Density of liquid is \(\rho_2\).

[EQUATION]
\text{Weight of the body}, w_B = V_1 \rho_1 g
[EQUATION]
\text{Upthrust of the fluid}, U = V_2 \rho_2 g
[PARAGRAPH]
From the principle of floatation: \(V_1 \rho_1 g = V_2 \rho_2 g\)

[EQUATION]
\frac{V_1}{V_2} = \frac{\rho_2}{\rho_1} \quad \implies \quad \frac{\text{volume of floating body}}{\text{volume of submerged part}} = \frac{\text{density of liquid}}{\text{density of body}}
[BEGIN EXAMPLE label="Example 5.6"]
[EXAMPLE]
Suppose an 80 kg man floats in fresh water with 92% of his volume submerged when his lungs are full of air. What is his average density? Take the density of water to be \(1000\,\mathrm{kg\,m^{-3}}\).

[SOLUTION]
\(\frac{V_1}{V_2} = \frac{\rho_{\text{water}}}{\rho_{\text{man}}} \implies \rho_{\text{man}} = \frac{V_2}{V_1} \times \rho_{\text{water}} = 0.92 \times 1000\,\mathrm{kg\,m^{-3}} = 920\,\mathrm{kg\,m^{-3}}\)
[END EXAMPLE]

[END_PAGE]

[PDF_PAGE 117]
[PRINTED_PAGE 111]

[RUNNING_HEADER]
Sinking and floating

[BEGIN EXAMPLE label="Example 5.7"]
[EXAMPLE]
Calculate the density of a wood, if a floating body is 98% submerged in water. Take the density of water to be \(1000\,\mathrm{kg\,m^{-3}}\).

[SOLUTION]
\(\rho_b = 0.98 \times 1000\,\mathrm{kg\,m^{-3}} = 980\,\mathrm{kg\,m^{-3}}\)
[END EXAMPLE]

[HEADING level=3 role=subsection]
Law of floatation in everyday life

[PARAGRAPH]
Balloons, canoes, ships, boats and submarines obey the law of flotation in their operations. These bodies are designed in such a way that the average density of the body is less than the density of the fluid in which they float.

[HEADING level=4 role=other]
Balloons and Hot-air balloons

[PARAGRAPH]
A hot-air balloon consists of an envelope that contains heated air. As the air inside expands, its density decreases below that of the surrounding cooler air, producing an upthrust that lifts the balloon.

[CAPTION label="Figure 5.11"]
Figure 5.11: Hot-air balloon

[END_PAGE]

[PDF_PAGE 118]
[PRINTED_PAGE 112]

[RUNNING_HEADER]
Physics for Secondary Schools

[BEGIN EXAMPLE label="Example 5.8"]
[EXAMPLE]
A balloon made up of a fabric weighing 80 N has a volume of \(1.0 \times 10^7\,\mathrm{cm^3}\). The balloon is filled with hydrogen of density \(0.9\,\mathrm{kg\,m^{-3}}\). Calculate the greatest weight in addition to that of the hydrogen and the fabric, which the balloon can carry in air of an average density \(1.25\,\mathrm{kg\,m^{-3}}\).

[SOLUTION]
\(\text{Upthrust} = \text{volume of air} \times \text{density of air} \times g = 10\,\mathrm{m^3} \times 1.25\,\mathrm{kg\,m^{-3}} \times 10\,\mathrm{N\,kg^{-1}} = 125\,\mathrm{N}\)
\(\text{Weight of hydrogen} = 0.90\,\mathrm{kg\,m^{-3}} \times 10\,\mathrm{m^3} \times 10\,\mathrm{N\,kg^{-1}} = 9\,\mathrm{N}\)
\(\text{Greatest added weight} = 125\,\mathrm{N} - 80\,\mathrm{N} - 9\,\mathrm{N} = 36\,\mathrm{N}\)
[END EXAMPLE]

[HEADING level=4 role=other]
Submarines

[PARAGRAPH]
Submarines use ballast tanks to control their average density. To sink, seawater is allowed into ballast tanks; to resurface, compressed air forces the water out.

[CAPTION label="Figure 5.12"]
Figure 5.12: Submarine

[END_PAGE]

[PDF_PAGE 119]
[PRINTED_PAGE 113]

[RUNNING_HEADER]
Sinking and floating

[HEADING level=4 role=other]
Ships and Plimsoll lines

[PARAGRAPH]
Ships have large hollow spaces so that their total average density is less than water. Special line markings called Plimsoll lines show the maximum safe loading limit in waters of different temperatures and salinities.

[CAPTION label="Figure 5.13"]
Figure 5.13: Plimsoll lines on a side of a ship

[HEADING level=3 role=subsection]
Hydrometer

[PARAGRAPH]
A hydrometer is an instrument used to measure the relative density of liquids. It consists of a graduated narrow glass stem, a wide bulb, and lead or mercury ballast at the bottom to float upright.

[CAPTION label="Figure 5.14"]
Figure 5.14: The Hydrometer

[END_PAGE]

[PDF_PAGE 120]
[PRINTED_PAGE 114]

[RUNNING_HEADER]
Physics for Secondary Schools

[CAPTION label="Figure 5.15"]
Figure 5.15: Hydrometer in water and in methanol

[PARAGRAPH]
A hydrometer floats freely in a liquid and sinks deeper in less dense liquids (like methanol, R.D 0.8) and less in denser liquids (like water, R.D 1.0).

[END_PAGE]

[PDF_PAGE 121]
[PRINTED_PAGE 115]

[RUNNING_HEADER]
Sinking and floating

[BEGIN ACTIVITY label="Activity 5.8"]
[HEADING level=3 role=other]
Activity 5.8

[OBJECTIVES]
Aim: To determine the relative density of a liquid using a test-tube hydrometer.

[PARAGRAPH]
Materials: Liquid, hydrometer, sand or lead ballast

[INSTRUCTION]
Procedure

[LIST style=numbered]
1. Take a thin-walled test tube of cross-section area A with a waterproofed graph paper strip.
2. Float the test tube vertically in water (\(\rho_1\)) with sand ballast, and record length submerged as \(L_1\).
3. Replace water with another liquid (\(\rho_2\)) and record submerged length as \(L_2\).
4. Since upthrusts are equal: \(A L_1 \rho_1 g = A L_2 \rho_2 g \implies \frac{L_1}{L_2} = \frac{\rho_2}{\rho_1}\).

[END ACTIVITY]

[BEGIN EXAMPLE label="Example 5.9"]
[EXAMPLE]
The hydrometer in Figure 5.16 measures liquid densities between \(1.0\,\mathrm{g\,cm^{-3}}\) and \(0.8\,\mathrm{g\,cm^{-3}}\). The cross-sectional area of stem is 0.5 cm² and height between 0.8 and 1.0 marks is 16 cm. Determine the volume below the 1.0 mark.

[SOLUTION]
\(V_2 = 0.5 \times 16 = 8\,\mathrm{cm^3}\).
By law of floatation: \(1.0 \times V_1 = 0.8 \times (V_1 + 8) \implies 0.2 V_1 = 6.4 \implies V_1 = 32\,\mathrm{cm^3}\).
[END EXAMPLE]

[END_PAGE]

[PDF_PAGE 122]
[PRINTED_PAGE 116]

[RUNNING_HEADER]
Physics for Secondary Schools

[BEGIN SUMMARY]
[HEADING level=2 role=section]
Chapter summary

[LIST style=numbered]
1. An object immersed partially or totally in a fluid experiences an upward force called upthrust or buoyant force.
2. Apparent loss in weight = weight in air - weight in fluid = upthrust.
3. Archimedes' principle: Upthrust = weight of fluid displaced.
4. Relative density of solid = \(\frac{\text{weight in air}}{\text{weight in air} - \text{weight in water}}\).
5. Relative density of liquid = \(\frac{\text{weight in air} - \text{weight in liquid}}{\text{weight in air} - \text{weight in water}}\).
6. Law of floatation: A floating body displaces its own weight of fluid.
7. Submarines, ships, hot-air balloons and hydrometers operate based on floatation and density principles.
[END SUMMARY]

[END_PAGE]

[HEADING level=1 role=chapter]
Chapter Six: Mechanical properties of matter

[PARAGRAPH]
Introduction: Study of mechanical properties of matter plays a significant role in physics and engineering. When constructing houses, bridges, and fly-overs, proper selection of materials based on their properties is required.

[HEADING level=2 role=section]
Concept of elasticity

[PARAGRAPH]
If you stretch and release a rubber band, it will return to its original shape and size. The ability of objects to return to its original shape after deformation is called elasticity. A body with this ability is said to be elastic.

[HEADING level=3 role=subsection]
Relationship between tension and extension of an elastic material

[PARAGRAPH]
When a metal is subjected to a force it undergoes deformation. The force causing deformation is tension, while the extent of deformation is extension. Materials that cannot exhibit elasticity are inelastic materials.

[CAPTION label="Figure 6.1"]
Figure 6.1: Elastic materials

[END_PAGE]

[PDF_PAGE 128]
[PRINTED_PAGE 122]

[RUNNING_HEADER]
Physics for Secondary Schools

[BEGIN ACTIVITY label="Activity 6.1"]
[HEADING level=3 role=other]
Activity 6.1

[OBJECTIVES]
Aim: To investigate the relationship between tension applied to an elastic material and extension.

[PARAGRAPH]
Materials: Coil spring, pointer, slotted masses, mass holder, metre rule, retort stand and clamp

[CAPTION label="Figure 6.2"]
Figure 6.2: Apparatus setup for Hooke's law

[INSTRUCTION]
Procedure

[LIST style=numbered]
1. Hang a coil spring on a retort stand as shown in Figure 6.2.
2. Attach the pointer at the free end of the spring and hang the mass holder.
3. Place a metre rule adjacent to the apparatus.
4. Record initial pointer position \(X_0\). Add masses (50g, 100g, 150g, 200g) and record corresponding extensions \(e = X_i - X_0\).

[END ACTIVITY]

[END_PAGE]

[PDF_PAGE 129]
[PRINTED_PAGE 123]

[RUNNING_HEADER]
Mechanical properties of matter

[CAPTION label="Figure 6.4"]
Figure 6.4: Relationship between tension and extension of a stretched spring

[PARAGRAPH]
For portion OA, tension is directly proportional to extension. Point B represents the elastic limit. Beyond B (region BC), plastic deformation occurs.

[END_PAGE]

[PDF_PAGE 130]
[PRINTED_PAGE 124]

[RUNNING_HEADER]
Physics for Secondary Schools

[BEGIN NOTE kind=definition]
Hooke's law states that: "Provided the elastic limit is not exceeded, the extension is directly proportional to the force applied".

[EQUATION]
F = k e \quad \implies \quad k = \frac{F}{e}
[PARAGRAPH]
The constant k is called the elastic force constant, measured in \(\mathrm{N\,m^{-1}}\).

[BEGIN EXERCISE label="Exercise 6.1"]
[HEADING level=3 role=other]
Exercise 6.1

[QUESTION label="1."]

[QUESTION label="2."]

[QUESTION label="3."]
A spring has \(k = 800\,\mathrm{N\,m^{-1}}\). If compressed by 30 cm, find resulting force.

[END EXERCISE]

[HEADING level=3 role=subsection]
Applications of elasticity in daily life

[PARAGRAPH]
At home: rubber gaskets, clothing, furniture springs, rubber bands, toys.
In transport: shock absorbers, rubber tyres, bridge support cables.
In industry: steel construction beams, conveyor belts, weighing scales.

[END_PAGE]

[PDF_PAGE 131]
[PRINTED_PAGE 125]

[RUNNING_HEADER]
Mechanical properties of matter

[HEADING level=2 role=section]
Adhesion and cohesion

[BEGIN NOTE kind=definition]
Cohesion refers to the force of attraction between molecules of the same type, whereas adhesion refers to the force of attraction between molecules of different kinds.

[CAPTION label="Figure 6.5"]
Figure 6.5: Shapes of menisci of mercury and water

[PARAGRAPH]
Water forms a concave meniscus in glass because adhesion between water and glass is stronger than cohesion between water molecules. Mercury forms a convex meniscus because cohesion between mercury molecules is stronger than adhesion with glass.

[END_PAGE]

[PDF_PAGE 132]
[PRINTED_PAGE 126]

[RUNNING_HEADER]
Physics for Secondary Schools

[BEGIN ACTIVITY label="Activity 6.2"]

[PARAGRAPH]
Materials: Water, glycerine, glass slides, cylinders and droppers

[INSTRUCTION]

[END ACTIVITY]

[HEADING level=3 role=subsection]
Applications of adhesion and cohesion

[LIST style=numbered]
1. Glue and sticky tape adhesives.
2. Cohesion in water transport in plants (xylem).
3. Ink sticking to paper during writing.
4. Cohesion causing fluid viscosity.

[END_PAGE]

[PDF_PAGE 133]

[RUNNING_HEADER]
Mechanical properties of matter

[HEADING level=2 role=section]
Surface tension

[BEGIN NOTE kind=definition]
Surface tension is defined as the property of the surface of a liquid that allows it to resist an external force due to the cohesive nature of its molecules.

[CAPTION label="Figure 6.6"]
Figure 6.6: Water strider walking on water

[CAPTION label="Figure 6.7"]
Figure 6.7: Paper clip floating on water surface

[CAPTION label="Figure 6.8"]
Figure 6.8: Molecular origin of surface tension

[END_PAGE]

[PDF_PAGE 134]
[PRINTED_PAGE 128]

[RUNNING_HEADER]
Physics for Secondary Schools

[HEADING level=3 role=other]
Activity 6.3

[OBJECTIVES]
Aim: To demonstrate surface tension in water using needles, paper clips, and detergents.

[INSTRUCTION]
Procedure: Place needle/paperclip on clean water (floats due to surface tension). Add detergent (needle sinks as detergent reduces surface tension).

[END ACTIVITY]

[END_PAGE]

[PDF_PAGE 135]
[PRINTED_PAGE 129]

[RUNNING_HEADER]
Mechanical properties of matter

[PARAGRAPH]
A surfactant (surface active agent) reduces the surface tension of liquids.

[BEGIN ACTIVITY label="Activity 6.4"]
[HEADING level=3 role=other]
Activity 6.4

[OBJECTIVES]
Aim: To demonstrate surface tension in a soap film.

[PARAGRAPH]
A wire loop with a thread loop is dipped in soap solution. When the soap film inside the thread loop is pierced with a pin, the thread is pulled outwards into a perfect circle by external surface tension.

[END ACTIVITY]

[HEADING level=3 role=subsection]
Factors affecting surface tension

[LIST style=numbered]
1. Nature of liquid (mercury has higher surface tension than water).
2. Impurities and detergents (lower surface tension).
3. Temperature (surface tension decreases as temperature increases).

[END_PAGE]

[PDF_PAGE 136]
[PRINTED_PAGE 130]

[RUNNING_HEADER]
Physics for Secondary Schools

[HEADING level=3 role=subsection]
Applications of surface tension

[LIST style=numbered]
1. Soap action in washing clothes.
2. Pouring oil on stagnant water to drown mosquito larvae.
3. Hot soup spreading better over the tongue.

[HEADING level=2 role=section]
Capillarity

[BEGIN NOTE kind=definition]
Capillarity is defined as a phenomenon in which liquid rises or falls in a narrow space such as a thin tube or in voids of porous material without external forces.

[END_PAGE]

[PDF_PAGE 137]
[PRINTED_PAGE 131]

[RUNNING_HEADER]
Mechanical properties of matter

[HEADING level=3 role=subsection]
Factors affecting capillarity

[PARAGRAPH]
1. Diameter of tube: Height of rise \(h\) is inversely proportional to tube diameter \(d\) (\(h \approx \frac{0.3}{d}\) cm for glass in water).
2. Nature of liquid: Water rises in glass (adhesion > cohesion), mercury depresses in glass (cohesion > adhesion).

[CAPTION label="Figure 6.14"]
Figure 6.14: Capillary rise in tubes of different diameters

[CAPTION label="Figure 6.15"]
Figure 6.15: Capillary rise (water) and depression (mercury)

[END_PAGE]

[PDF_PAGE 138]
[PRINTED_PAGE 132]

[RUNNING_HEADER]
Physics for Secondary Schools

[HEADING level=3 role=subsection]
Applications of capillarity

[LIST style=numbered]
1. Water and mineral transport in plant roots and stems.
2. Kerosene drawing up the wick of a lamp.
3. Towels absorbing water from skin.
4. Blotting paper absorbing ink.

[END_PAGE]

[PDF_PAGE 139]
[PRINTED_PAGE 133]

[RUNNING_HEADER]
Mechanical properties of matter

[BEGIN SUMMARY]
[HEADING level=2 role=section]
Chapter summary

[LIST style=numbered]
1. Elasticity is ability to regain original shape after removing deforming force.
2. Hooke's law: \(F = k e\) within elastic limit.
3. Surface tension behaves like a stretched elastic skin due to cohesive forces.
4. Capillarity is rise or fall of liquids in narrow tubes caused by adhesion and cohesion.
[END SUMMARY]

[BEGIN EXERCISE label="Revision exercise 6"]
[HEADING level=2 role=section]
Revision exercise 6

[HEADING level=3 role=other]
Section A

[QUESTION label="1."]
(i) Limit of proportionality on load-extension graph is point P.
(ii) Material returning to original shape undergoes elastic deformation.
(iii) Water level higher inside glass capillary tube due to adhesion.
(iv) A weight of 2 N causes 6 cm extension; an 8 N weight causes 24 cm extension.
(v) Reducing capillary tube radius increases capillary rise.

[END_PAGE]

(i) Stretch \(e = \frac{5}{20} = 0.25\,\mathrm{m} = 25\,\mathrm{cm}\).

[HEADING level=1 role=chapter]
Chapter Seven: Pressure

[PARAGRAPH]
Introduction: Pressure influences daily life. Sharp edges of knives cut easily, wide shoulder pads on bags reduce shoulder pain, and wide foundations support tall buildings.

[HEADING level=2 role=section]
Concept of pressure

[CAPTION label="Figure 7.1"]
Figure 7.1: Driving an iron nail into wood via sharp end versus flat end

[CAPTION label="Figure 7.2"]
Figure 7.2: Driving wooden blocks of different surface areas into soft surface

[END_PAGE]

[PDF_PAGE 143]
[PRINTED_PAGE 137]

[RUNNING_HEADER]
Pressure

[BEGIN NOTE kind=definition]
Pressure is defined as the force acting normally or perpendicularly per unit surface area of an object.

[EQUATION]
P = \frac{F}{A}
[PARAGRAPH]
The SI unit of pressure is newton per square metre (\(\mathrm{N\,m^{-2}}\)) or Pascal (Pa).
Other units: 1 atm = \(1.01 \times 10^5\,\mathrm{Pa}\) = 760 mmHg = 1 bar = 760 torr.

[BEGIN ACTIVITY label="Activity 7.1"]
[HEADING level=3 role=other]
Activity 7.1

[OBJECTIVES]
Aim: To demonstrate the concept of pressure using buckets with thin versus thick handles.

[PARAGRAPH]
Lifting a heavy bucket with a thin handle exerts higher pressure on the hand causing pain; a thick handle increases surface area and reduces pressure.

[END ACTIVITY]

[END_PAGE]

[PDF_PAGE 144]
[PRINTED_PAGE 138]

[RUNNING_HEADER]
Physics for Secondary Schools

[HEADING level=2 role=section]
Pressure due to solids

[EQUATION]
\text{Pressure due to solid} = \frac{\text{Applied force}}{\text{Area of contact}} = \frac{F}{A}
[BEGIN EXAMPLE label="Example 7.1"]
[EXAMPLE]
A wooden block weighs 3 N and measures \(5\,\mathrm{cm} \times 10\,\mathrm{cm} \times 4\,\mathrm{cm}\).
(a) Largest base area = \(10\,\mathrm{cm} \times 5\,\mathrm{cm} = 0.005\,\mathrm{m^2} \implies P = \frac{3}{0.005} = 600\,\mathrm{N\,m^{-2}}\).
(b) Smallest base area = \(4\,\mathrm{cm} \times 5\,\mathrm{cm} = 0.002\,\mathrm{m^2} \implies P = \frac{3}{0.002} = 1500\,\mathrm{N\,m^{-2}}\).
[END EXAMPLE]

[BEGIN EXAMPLE label="Example 7.2"]
[EXAMPLE]
A needle tip area is \(1 \times 10^{-6}\,\mathrm{m^2}\). If a force of 20 N is applied, find pressure.
\(P = \frac{20}{1 \times 10^{-6}} = 2 \times 10^7\,\mathrm{Pa}\).
[END EXAMPLE]

[END_PAGE]

[PDF_PAGE 145]
[PRINTED_PAGE 139]

[RUNNING_HEADER]
Pressure

[HEADING level=3 role=subsection]
Applications of pressure due to solids

[LIST style=numbered]
1. Sharp tips on nails, pins, needles, and screws to increase penetration pressure.
2. Sharp cutting edges on knives, axes, and razor blades.
3. Defensive sharp fins on fish.

[BEGIN EXERCISE label="Exercise 7.1"]
[HEADING level=3 role=other]
Exercise 7.1

[QUESTION label="1."]
A plastic block of mass 1.5 kg (\(15\,\mathrm{N}\)) measures \(40\,\mathrm{cm} \times 30\,\mathrm{cm} \times 20\,\mathrm{cm}\). Calculate greatest and least pressure on floor.
Greatest \(P = \frac{15}{0.06} = 250\,\mathrm{Pa}\); Least \(P = \frac{15}{0.12} = 125\,\mathrm{Pa}\).

[QUESTION label="2."]
Student mass 43.8 kg (\(438\,\mathrm{N}\)), foot area 8 cm² (\(0.0008\,\mathrm{m^2}\)). Pressure = \(\frac{438}{0.0008} = 547500\,\mathrm{Pa}\).

[END EXERCISE]

[PDF_PAGE 147]
[PRINTED_PAGE 141]

[RUNNING_HEADER]
Pressure

[HEADING level=2 role=section]
Pressure in liquids

[PARAGRAPH]
The pressure in a liquid column of height \(h\), density \(\rho\), under gravity \(g\) is derived as:

[EQUATION]
\text{Volume} = A h, \quad \text{Mass} = A h \rho, \quad \text{Weight } (F) = A h \rho g
[EQUATION]
P = \frac{F}{A} = \frac{A h \rho g}{A} = h \rho g
[PARAGRAPH]
Pressure in liquids is independent of cross-sectional area or container shape.

[END_PAGE]

[PDF_PAGE 148]
[PRINTED_PAGE 142]

[RUNNING_HEADER]
Physics for Secondary Schools

[HEADING level=3 role=subsection]
Characteristics of pressure in liquids

[LIST style=numbered]
1. Pressure in a liquid increases with depth (\(P \propto h\)).
2. Pressure acts equally in all directions at a given depth.
3. Pressure increases with liquid density (\(P \propto \rho\)).

[BEGIN ACTIVITY label="Activity 7.3"]
[HEADING level=3 role=other]
Activity 7.3

[OBJECTIVES]
Aim: To demonstrate variation of liquid pressure with depth using a tall container with holes at different heights.

[PARAGRAPH]
Water spurts out furthest from the bottom hole because pressure is highest at the greatest depth.

[END ACTIVITY]

[END_PAGE]

[PDF_PAGE 149]
[PRINTED_PAGE 143]

[RUNNING_HEADER]
Physics for Secondary Schools

[HEADING level=3 role=subsection]
Pressure in liquid and upthrust

[CAPTION label="Figure 7.16"]
Figure 7.16: Variation in liquid pressure producing upthrust

[PARAGRAPH]
Horizontal pressures cancel out, while bottom upward pressure exceeds top downward pressure, creating net upward force (upthrust).

[BEGIN EXAMPLE label="Example 7.3"]
[EXAMPLE]
A 2 cm cube is submerged in liquid (\(\rho = 1027\,\mathrm{kg\,m^{-3}}\)) with bottom at 10 cm depth.
(a) Bottom pressure = \(0.10 \times 1027 \times 10 = 1027\,\mathrm{Pa}\); Top pressure (8cm) = \(0.08 \times 1027 \times 10 = 821.6\,\mathrm{Pa}\). Pressure diff = 205.4 Pa.
(b) Force diff = \(205.4 \times (0.02)^2 = 0.0822\,\mathrm{N}\).
(c) Displaced water weight = \(1027 \times 8 \times 10^{-6} \times 10 = 0.0822\,\mathrm{N}\).
[END EXAMPLE]

[END_PAGE]

[PDF_PAGE 151]
[PRINTED_PAGE 145]

[RUNNING_HEADER]
Pressure

[HEADING level=3 role=subsection]
Pascal's principle of transmission of pressure in liquids

[PARAGRAPH]
Pascal's principle states that when pressure is applied to an enclosed fluid, it is transmitted undiminished to every portion of the fluid and to the walls of the containing vessel.

[BEGIN ACTIVITY label="Activity 7.4"]
[INSTRUCTION]
Investigating transmission of pressure in liquids.

[LIST style=numbered]
1. Take a round-bottomed glass flask fitted with a stopper and small equal-sized holes around its body.
2. Fill the flask completely with water.
3. Push the plunger inwards firmly.

[PARAGRAPH]
Observation: Water spurts out equally in all directions through all the holes with equal force, demonstrating that pressure applied at one point is transmitted equally throughout the liquid.
[END ACTIVITY]

[HEADING level=3 role=subsection]
Applications of Pascal's principle

[HEADING level=4 role=other]
1. Hydraulic press

[PARAGRAPH]
A hydraulic press consists of two cylinders of different cross-sectional areas connected by a pipe containing a liquid (such as oil or water). A small force \(F_1\) applied to the small piston of area \(A_1\) creates a pressure \(P_1 = \frac{F_1}{A_1}\).

[PARAGRAPH]
According to Pascal's principle, this pressure is transmitted undiminished to the larger piston of area \(A_2\), producing a force \(F_2\) given by:

[EQUATION]
P_2 = P_1 \implies \frac{F_2}{A_2} = \frac{F_1}{A_1}
[EQUATION]
F_2 = F_1 \times \frac{A_2}{A_1}
[PARAGRAPH]
Since \(A_2 > A_1\), the output force \(F_2\) is much larger than the input force \(F_1\). Therefore, a hydraulic press acts as a force multiplier.

[BEGIN EXAMPLE label="Example 7.6"]
[PARAGRAPH]
In a hydraulic press, a force of \(20\,\mathrm{N}\) is applied to a small piston of cross-sectional area \(0.02\,\mathrm{m^2}\). Calculate the force exerted on the large piston if its cross-sectional area is \(0.8\,\mathrm{m^2}\).

[SOLUTION]
Given:
Input force, \(F_1 = 20\,\mathrm{N}\)
Area of small piston, \(A_1 = 0.02\,\mathrm{m^2}\)
Area of large piston, \(A_2 = 0.8\,\mathrm{m^2}\)

From Pascal's principle:
[EQUATION]
\frac{F_1}{A_1} = \frac{F_2}{A_2}
[EQUATION]
F_2 = F_1 \times \frac{A_2}{A_1} = 20\,\mathrm{N} \times \frac{0.8\,\mathrm{m^2}}{0.02\,\mathrm{m^2}} = 800\,\mathrm{N}

[PARAGRAPH]
The force exerted on the large piston is \(800\,\mathrm{N}\).

[END EXAMPLE]

[END_PAGE]

[PDF_PAGE 152]
[PRINTED_PAGE 146]

[RUNNING_HEADER]
Pressure

[HEADING level=4 role=other]
2. Hydraulic brakes

[PARAGRAPH]
Hydraulic brakes in motor vehicles use Pascal's principle to apply equal braking force to all wheels simultaneously. When the driver presses the brake pedal, a small force pushes the master piston, increasing the hydraulic fluid pressure in the brake lines. This pressure is transmitted undiminished to the slave cylinders at each wheel, pushing the brake pads against the brake discs or drums to slow down or stop the vehicle.

[HEADING level=2 role=section]
Atmospheric pressure

[PARAGRAPH]
Atmospheric pressure is the force per unit area exerted against a surface by the weight of the air column above that surface in the Earth's atmosphere. At sea level, standard atmospheric pressure is approximately \(101,325\,\mathrm{Pa}\) or \(1.013 \times 10^5\,\mathrm{Pa}\) (often rounded to \(1.0 \times 10^5\,\mathrm{Pa}\) or \(760\,\mathrm{mmHg}\)).

[BEGIN ACTIVITY label="Activity 7.5"]
[INSTRUCTION]
Demonstrating the existence of atmospheric pressure using the crushing can experiment.

[LIST style=numbered]
1. Pour a small amount of water into a tin can with a tight-fitting lid left open.
2. Heat the water until it boils and steam escapes freely, driving out most of the air inside.
3. Remove the can from heat, tightly seal the lid, and pour cold water over the sealed can.

[PARAGRAPH]
Observation: The can immediately collapses and crushes inward.
Explanation: The cold water condenses the steam inside into water, creating a partial vacuum. The atmospheric pressure outside the can is much greater than the pressure inside, causing the can to be crushed.
[END ACTIVITY]

[END_PAGE]

[PDF_PAGE 153]
[PRINTED_PAGE 147]

[RUNNING_HEADER]
Pressure

[HEADING level=3 role=subsection]
Measurement of atmospheric pressure

[PARAGRAPH]
Atmospheric pressure is measured using instruments called barometers.

[HEADING level=4 role=other]
1. Simple mercury barometer

[PARAGRAPH]
A simple mercury barometer consists of a glass tube about \(1\,\mathrm{m}\) long, closed at one end, filled completely with mercury, and inverted into a trough of mercury. The mercury column falls until its height is supported by the atmospheric pressure acting on the open surface of mercury in the trough.

[PARAGRAPH]
At standard atmospheric pressure at sea level, the vertical height \(h\) of the mercury column is \(760\,\mathrm{mm}\) (or \(0.76\,\mathrm{m}\)). The space above the mercury column inside the tube is a vacuum, known as a Torricellian vacuum.

[EQUATION]
P_{\mathrm{atm}} = h \rho g
[PARAGRAPH]
Where \(h = 0.76\,\mathrm{m}\), \(\rho = 13,600\,\mathrm{kg\,m^{-3}}\), and \(g = 9.8\,\mathrm{N\,kg^{-1}}\):

[EQUATION]
P_{\mathrm{atm}} = 0.76\,\mathrm{m} \times 13,600\,\mathrm{kg\,m^{-3}} \times 9.8\,\mathrm{N\,kg^{-1}} \approx 1.013 \times 10^5\,\mathrm{Pa}
[BEGIN NOTE kind=definition]
Mercury is preferred over water in barometers because of its high density (\(13,600\,\mathrm{kg\,m^{-3}}\)), requiring a tube length of less than \(1\,\mathrm{m}\). If water were used, a tube height of over \(10.3\,\mathrm{m}\) would be required because \(h_{\mathrm{water}} = \frac{1.013 \times 10^5}{1000 \times 9.8} \approx 10.34\,\mathrm{m}\).
[END NOTE]

[END_PAGE]

[PDF_PAGE 154]
[PRINTED_PAGE 148]

[RUNNING_HEADER]
Pressure

[HEADING level=4 role=other]
2. Fortin barometer

[PARAGRAPH]
The Fortin barometer is a refined and highly accurate version of the simple mercury barometer. It features an ivory index point and a flexible leather bag bottom whose level can be adjusted using a thumbscrew so that the mercury surface barely touches the ivory pointer before taking a reading. A vernier scale mounted on the frame allows accurate readings down to \(0.1\,\mathrm{mm}\).

[HEADING level=4 role=other]
3. Aneroid barometer

[PARAGRAPH]
An aneroid barometer is a portable instrument that measures atmospheric pressure without using any liquid. It consists of an evacuated metallic box with flexible corrugated sides. Changes in atmospheric pressure cause the box to expand or contract. This movement is transmitted through a mechanical lever system to a pointer moving across a calibrated circular scale.

[HEADING level=3 role=subsection]
Applications of atmospheric pressure

[PARAGRAPH]
Atmospheric pressure plays an essential role in everyday devices and machines:

[LIST style=bullet]
* **Syringe:** Pulling the plunger increases internal volume and reduces internal pressure, allowing external atmospheric pressure to force liquid up into the barrel.
* **Drinking straw:** Sucking air through a straw reduces air pressure inside it, allowing atmospheric pressure acting on the liquid surface to push liquid up the straw.
* **Siphon:** A tube used to transfer liquid from a higher container over an obstacle to a lower container continuously using atmospheric pressure and gravity differentials.
* **Lift pump and Force pump:** Piston pumps using atmospheric pressure and non-return valves to raise water from wells.

[END_PAGE]

[PDF_PAGE 155]
[PRINTED_PAGE 149]

[RUNNING_HEADER]
Pressure

[BEGIN ACTIVITY label="Activity 7.6"]
[INSTRUCTION]
Demonstrating the operation of a siphon.

[LIST style=numbered]
1. Fill a flexible plastic tube completely with water.
2. Pinch both ends of the tube to prevent water from spilling out.
3. Place one end in a beaker of water placed on an elevated table, and submerge the other end into an empty beaker placed at a lower level on the floor.
4. Release both ends simultaneously.

[PARAGRAPH]
Observation: Water flows continuously from the upper beaker to the lower beaker until the upper container empties or the water levels equalize.
[END ACTIVITY]

[BEGIN SUMMARY]
[HEADING level=3 role=other]
Summary of Chapter Seven
[LIST style=bullet]
* Pressure is defined as the force acting normally per unit area: \(P = \frac{F}{A}\).
* The SI unit of pressure is the Pascal (\(\mathrm{Pa}\)), where \(1\,\mathrm{Pa} = 1\,\mathrm{N\,m^{-2}}\).
* Pressure in liquids depends on depth, liquid density, and acceleration due to gravity: \(P = h \rho g\).
* Liquid pressure acts equally in all directions at the same depth and is independent of the shape or size of the container.
* Pascal's principle states that pressure applied to an enclosed fluid is transmitted undiminished to every part of the fluid.
* Atmospheric pressure is the pressure exerted by the weight of air in the atmosphere, measured using barometers (simple mercury, Fortin, aneroid).
[END SUMMARY]

[END_PAGE]

[PRINTED_PAGE 150]

[HEADING level=1 role=chapter]
Chapter Eight: Work, Energy and Power

[HEADING level=2 role=section]
Concept of work

[PARAGRAPH]
In everyday language, "work" refers to any mental or physical effort. In physics, however, work has a precise scientific definition. Work is done whenever a force acts on an object and causes that object to move through a distance in the direction of the force.

[PARAGRAPH]
If no displacement occurs, or if the direction of motion is perpendicular to the applied force, no work is done in the physical sense. For example, pushing against a stationary wall or holding a heavy suitcase stationary requires force and energy, but zero mechanical work is done on the wall or suitcase.

[HEADING level=3 role=subsection]
Formula and unit of work

[PARAGRAPH]
Work (\(W\)) is calculated as the product of the magnitude of the force (\(F\)) and the distance (\(d\)) moved by the object in the direction of the force:

[EQUATION]
W = F \times d
[PARAGRAPH]
Where:
- \(W\) is the work done in Joules (\(\mathrm{J}\))
- \(F\) is the constant force in Newtons (\(\mathrm{N}\))
- \(d\) is the displacement in metres (\(\mathrm{m}\))

[BEGIN NOTE kind=definition]
The SI unit of work is the **Joule** (\(\mathrm{J}\)). One Joule is defined as the work done when a force of one Newton moves an object through a distance of one metre in the direction of the force:
[EQUATION]
1\,\mathrm{J} = 1\,\mathrm{N\,m} = 1\,\mathrm{kg\,m^2\,s^{-2}}
[END NOTE]

[END_PAGE]

[PDF_PAGE 158]
[PRINTED_PAGE 152]

[RUNNING_HEADER]
Work, Energy and Power

[BEGIN EXAMPLE label="Example 8.1"]
[PARAGRAPH]
A horizontal force of \(150\,\mathrm{N}\) is applied to push a box across a smooth floor through a distance of \(8\,\mathrm{m}\). Calculate the work done on the box.

[SOLUTION]
Given:
Force, \(F = 150\,\mathrm{N}\)
Distance, \(d = 8\,\mathrm{m}\)

Work done, \(W\):
[EQUATION]
W = F \times d = 150\,\mathrm{N} \times 8\,\mathrm{m} = 1200\,\mathrm{J}

[PARAGRAPH]
The work done on the box is \(1200\,\mathrm{J}\) (or \(1.2\,\mathrm{kJ}\)).

[END EXAMPLE]

[BEGIN EXAMPLE label="Example 8.2"]
[PARAGRAPH]
A crane lifts a load of mass \(400\,\mathrm{kg}\) vertically upwards through a height of \(15\,\mathrm{m}\) at a constant speed. Calculate the work done against gravity. (Take \(g = 9.8\,\mathrm{N\,kg^{-1}}\)).

[SOLUTION]
Given:
Mass, \(m = 400\,\mathrm{kg}\)
Height, \(h = 15\,\mathrm{m}\)
Force required to lift load, \(F = mg = 400\,\mathrm{kg} \times 9.8\,\mathrm{N\,kg^{-1}} = 3920\,\mathrm{N}\)

Work done against gravity:
[EQUATION]
W = F \times h = 3920\,\mathrm{N} \times 15\,\mathrm{m} = 58,800\,\mathrm{J} = 58.8\,\mathrm{kJ}

[PARAGRAPH]
The work done by the crane is \(58.8\,\mathrm{kJ}\).

[END EXAMPLE]

[HEADING level=2 role=section]
Concept of energy

[PARAGRAPH]
Energy is defined as the capacity or ability to do work. Whenever work is done on an object, energy is transferred to or transformed within that object. Energy is a scalar quantity and shares the same SI unit as work, the **Joule** (\(\mathrm{J}\)).

[END_PAGE]

[PDF_PAGE 159]
[PRINTED_PAGE 153]

[RUNNING_HEADER]
Work, Energy and Power

[HEADING level=3 role=subsection]
Forms of energy

[PARAGRAPH]
Energy exists in various forms in nature, including:

[LIST style=bullet]
* **Mechanical energy:** The energy possessed by an object due to its motion or position (comprising kinetic energy and potential energy).
* **Chemical energy:** Energy stored in the chemical bonds of atoms and molecules (found in food, fuels, batteries, and explosives).
* **Thermal (Heat) energy:** Energy resulting from the microscopic motion and vibrations of particles within a substance.
* **Electrical energy:** Energy associated with the movement of electric charges (electrons) through a conductor.
* **Sound energy:** Energy produced by vibrating objects and transmitted through a medium as pressure waves.
* **Light (Radiant) energy:** Electromagnetic radiation visible to the human eye, traveling in transverse waves.
* **Nuclear energy:** Energy stored in the atomic nucleus, released during nuclear fission or fusion reactions.

[HEADING level=3 role=subsection]
Mechanical energy: Potential and Kinetic energy

[HEADING level=4 role=other]
1. Potential Energy (PE)

[PARAGRAPH]
Potential energy is the energy possessed by a body or system due to its position, state, or configuration.

[PARAGRAPH]
Gravitational Potential Energy (GPE) is the energy stored in an object elevated above the surface of the Earth. When a body of mass \(m\) is raised through a vertical height \(h\) against gravitational force \(g\), the work done on the object is stored as gravitational potential energy:

[EQUATION]
E_p = mgh
[END_PAGE]

[PDF_PAGE 160]
[PRINTED_PAGE 154]

[RUNNING_HEADER]
Work, Energy and Power

[BEGIN EXAMPLE label="Example 8.3"]
[PARAGRAPH]
A stone of mass \(2.5\,\mathrm{kg}\) is placed on a shelf \(4\,\mathrm{m}\) above the ground. Calculate its gravitational potential energy relative to the ground. (Take \(g = 9.8\,\mathrm{N\,kg^{-1}}\)).

[SOLUTION]
Given:
Mass, \(m = 2.5\,\mathrm{kg}\)
Height, \(h = 4\,\mathrm{m}\)
Acceleration due to gravity, \(g = 9.8\,\mathrm{N\,kg^{-1}}\)

Potential energy, \(E_p\):
[EQUATION]
E_p = mgh = 2.5\,\mathrm{kg} \times 9.8\,\mathrm{N\,kg^{-1}} \times 4\,\mathrm{m} = 98\,\mathrm{J}

[PARAGRAPH]
The gravitational potential energy of the stone is \(98\,\mathrm{J}\).

[END EXAMPLE]

[HEADING level=4 role=other]
2. Kinetic Energy (KE)

[PARAGRAPH]
Kinetic energy is the energy possessed by a body due to its motion. Any moving object of mass \(m\) moving with velocity \(v\) possesses kinetic energy \(E_k\) given by the formula:

[EQUATION]
E_k = \frac{1}{2} m v^2
[BEGIN EXAMPLE label="Example 8.4"]
[PARAGRAPH]
A car of mass \(1200\,\mathrm{kg}\) is travelling along a straight horizontal road at a speed of \(20\,\mathrm{m\,s^{-1}}\). Calculate its kinetic energy.

[SOLUTION]
Given:
Mass, \(m = 1200\,\mathrm{kg}\)
Speed, \(v = 20\,\mathrm{m\,s^{-1}}\)

Kinetic energy, \(E_k\):
[EQUATION]
E_k = \frac{1}{2} m v^2 = \frac{1}{2} \times 1200\,\mathrm{kg} \times (20\,\mathrm{m\,s^{-1}})^2
[EQUATION]
E_k = 600 \times 400 = 240,000\,\mathrm{J} = 240\,\mathrm{kJ}

[PARAGRAPH]
The kinetic energy of the car is \(240\,\mathrm{kJ}\).

[END EXAMPLE]

[END_PAGE]

[PDF_PAGE 161]
[PRINTED_PAGE 155]

[RUNNING_HEADER]
Work, Energy and Power

[HEADING level=3 role=subsection]
Principle of conservation of energy

[PARAGRAPH]
The principle of conservation of energy states that energy cannot be created or destroyed; it can only be transformed from one form into another. The total amount of energy in an isolated system remains constant.

[PARAGRAPH]
For a freely falling object of mass \(m\) dropped from height \(H\) under gravity (ignoring air resistance):

[LIST style=bullet]
* At the initial top position (height \(H\)): \(E_p = mgH\), \(E_k = 0\). Total energy = \(mgH\).
* At any intermediate height \(h\): \(E_p = mgh\), \(E_k = mg(H - h)\). Total energy = \(mgh + mg(H - h) = mgH\).
* Just before striking the ground (height = \(0\)): \(E_p = 0\), \(E_k = \frac{1}{2}mv^2 = mgH\). Total energy = \(mgH\).

[BEGIN EXAMPLE label="Example 8.5"]
[PARAGRAPH]
A coconut of mass \(0.8\,\mathrm{kg}\) falls freely from a palm tree from a height of \(10\,\mathrm{m}\) above the ground.
[QUESTION label="(a)"]
Calculate its potential energy before falling.
[QUESTION label="(b)"]
Determine its kinetic energy and velocity just before hitting the ground. (Take \(g = 9.8\,\mathrm{N\,kg^{-1}}\)).

[SOLUTION]
(a) Potential energy at height \(h = 10\,\mathrm{m}\):
[EQUATION]
E_p = mgh = 0.8\,\mathrm{kg} \times 9.8\,\mathrm{N\,kg^{-1}} \times 10\,\mathrm{m} = 78.4\,\mathrm{J}

[PARAGRAPH]
(b) By conservation of energy, kinetic energy at the bottom equals initial potential energy:

[EQUATION]
E_k = 78.4\,\mathrm{J}

[PARAGRAPH]
To find final velocity \(v\):

[EQUATION]
\frac{1}{2} m v^2 = E_k \implies v = \sqrt{\frac{2 E_k}{m}} = \sqrt{\frac{2 \times 78.4}{0.8}} = \sqrt{196} = 14\,\mathrm{m\,s^{-1}}

[PARAGRAPH]
The coconut hits the ground with a speed of \(14\,\mathrm{m\,s^{-1}}\).

[END EXAMPLE]

[END_PAGE]

[PDF_PAGE 162]
[PRINTED_PAGE 156]

[RUNNING_HEADER]
Work, Energy and Power

[HEADING level=3 role=subsection]
Energy sources and transformations

[PARAGRAPH]
Energy sources are broadly classified into two major categories:

[HEADING level=4 role=other]
1. Renewable energy sources

[PARAGRAPH]
Renewable energy sources are natural energy resources that replenish naturally over short time scales and will not deplete with continued use.

[LIST style=bullet]
* **Solar energy:** Radiant light and heat from the Sun, harnessed using photovoltaic cells or solar thermal collectors.
* **Wind energy:** Kinetic energy of moving air masses turning wind turbines to generate electricity.
* **Hydroelectric energy:** Gravitational potential energy of stored river or dam water converted into electrical energy via turbines.
* **Geothermal energy:** Thermal energy generated and stored in the Earth's crust (hot springs, geysers, steam reservoirs).
* **Biomass energy:** Energy derived from organic plant and animal waste matter (wood, biogas, ethanol).

[HEADING level=4 role=other]
2. Non-renewable energy sources

[PARAGRAPH]
Non-renewable energy sources exist in finite amounts and take millions of years to form; they deplete as they are consumed.

[LIST style=bullet]
* **Fossil fuels:** Coal, crude oil (petroleum), and natural gas formed from ancient organic matter under intense pressure and heat.
* **Nuclear fuels:** Radioactive elements such as uranium-235 and thorium used in nuclear power reactors.

[END_PAGE]

[PDF_PAGE 163]
[PRINTED_PAGE 157]

[RUNNING_HEADER]
Work, Energy and Power

[HEADING level=2 role=section]
Concept of power

[PARAGRAPH]
In physics, power is defined as the rate of doing work or the rate at which energy is transferred or transformed.

[HEADING level=3 role=subsection]
Formula and unit of power

[PARAGRAPH]
Power (\(P\)) is calculated by dividing the work done (\(W\)) or energy transferred (\(E\)) by the time taken (\(t\)):

[EQUATION]
P = \frac{W}{t} = \frac{E}{t}
[PARAGRAPH]
Where:
- \(P\) is power in Watts (\(\mathrm{W}\))
- \(W\) is work done in Joules (\(\mathrm{J}\))
- \(t\) is time taken in seconds (\(\mathrm{s}\))

[BEGIN NOTE kind=definition]
The SI unit of power is the **Watt** (\(\mathrm{W}\)), named after Scottish engineer James Watt. One Watt is defined as the power developed when work is done at the rate of one Joule per second:
[EQUATION]
1\,\mathrm{W} = 1\,\mathrm{J\,s^{-1}} = 1\,\mathrm{kg\,m^2\,s^{-3}}
[END NOTE]

[PARAGRAPH]
Larger units of power commonly used in engineering include:
- Kilowatt (\(\mathrm{kW}\)): \(1\,\mathrm{kW} = 10^3\,\mathrm{W} = 1,000\,\mathrm{W}\)
- Megawatt (\(\mathrm{MW}\)): \(1\,\mathrm{MW} = 10^6\,\mathrm{W} = 1,000,000\,\mathrm{W}\)
- Horsepower (\(\mathrm{hp}\)): \(1\,\mathrm{hp} \approx 746\,\mathrm{W}\)

[END_PAGE]

[PDF_PAGE 164]
[PRINTED_PAGE 158]

[RUNNING_HEADER]
Work, Energy and Power

[BEGIN EXAMPLE label="Example 8.6"]
[PARAGRAPH]
An electric motor performs \(45,000\,\mathrm{J}\) of work in \(30\,\mathrm{seconds}\). Calculate the power output of the motor in Watts and Kilowatts.

[SOLUTION]
Given:
Work done, \(W = 45,000\,\mathrm{J}\)
Time, \(t = 30\,\mathrm{s}\)

Power, \(P\):
[EQUATION]
P = \frac{W}{t} = \frac{45,000\,\mathrm{J}}{30\,\mathrm{s}} = 1500\,\mathrm{W}

[PARAGRAPH]
In Kilowatts:

[EQUATION]
P = \frac{1500\,\mathrm{W}}{1000} = 1.5\,\mathrm{kW}

[PARAGRAPH]
The power output of the motor is \(1500\,\mathrm{W}\) (or \(1.5\,\mathrm{kW}\)).

[END EXAMPLE]

[BEGIN EXAMPLE label="Example 8.7"]
[PARAGRAPH]
A student of mass \(50\,\mathrm{kg}\) runs up a flight of stairs of vertical height \(6\,\mathrm{m}\) in \(5\,\mathrm{seconds}\). Calculate the average power generated by the student. (Take \(g = 9.8\,\mathrm{N\,kg^{-1}}\)).

[SOLUTION]
Given:
Mass, \(m = 50\,\mathrm{kg}\)
Height, \(h = 6\,\mathrm{m}\)
Time, \(t = 5\,\mathrm{s}\)

Work done against gravity:
[EQUATION]
W = mgh = 50\,\mathrm{kg} \times 9.8\,\mathrm{N\,kg^{-1}} \times 6\,\mathrm{m} = 2940\,\mathrm{J}

[PARAGRAPH]
Power developed:

[EQUATION]
P = \frac{W}{t} = \frac{2940\,\mathrm{J}}{5\,\mathrm{s}} = 588\,\mathrm{W}

[PARAGRAPH]
The student develops an average power of \(588\,\mathrm{W}\).

[END EXAMPLE]

[END_PAGE]

[PDF_PAGE 165]
[PRINTED_PAGE 159]

[RUNNING_HEADER]
Work, Energy and Power

[BEGIN SUMMARY]
[HEADING level=3 role=other]
Summary of Chapter Eight
[LIST style=bullet]
* Work is done when a force produces motion in the direction of the force: \(W = F \times d\).
* The SI unit of work and energy is the Joule (\(\mathrm{J}\)).
* Energy is the capacity to do work.
* Mechanical energy consists of potential energy (\(E_p = mgh\)) and kinetic energy (\(E_k = \frac{1}{2}mv^2\)).
* The principle of conservation of energy states that energy cannot be created or destroyed, only transformed.
* Renewable energy sources (sun, wind, water, geothermal, biomass) replenish naturally; non-renewable sources (fossil fuels, nuclear fuels) deplete.
* Power is the rate of doing work or transferring energy: \(P = \frac{W}{t}\), measured in Watts (\(\mathrm{W}\)).
[END SUMMARY]

[END_PAGE]

[PDF_PAGE 166]
[PRINTED_PAGE 160]

Work, Energy and Power

[HEADING level=1 role=chapter]
Chapter Nine: Light

[HEADING level=2 role=section]
Concept and sources of light

[PARAGRAPH]
Light is a form of radiant energy that stimulates the sense of sight in the human eye, enabling us to see surrounding objects. Light travels as transverse electromagnetic waves and does not require a material medium for propagation. In a vacuum, light travels at a constant speed of approximately \(c = 3.0 \times 10^8\,\mathrm{m\,s^{-1}}\).

[HEADING level=3 role=subsection]
Luminous and non-luminous bodies

[PARAGRAPH]
Objects around us are classified based on their ability to emit light:

[LIST style=bullet]
* **Luminous bodies:** Objects that produce and emit their own light (e.g., the Sun, stars, burning candles, electric bulbs, fireflies).
* **Non-luminous bodies:** Objects that do not emit light of their own but become visible when they reflect light falling on them from a luminous source (e.g., the Moon, planets, tables, books, mirrors).

[HEADING level=3 role=subsection]
Transparent, translucent and opaque materials

[PARAGRAPH]
Materials interact differently when light falls upon them:

[LIST style=bullet]
* **Transparent materials:** Materials that allow almost all light to pass through them easily, enabling objects behind them to be seen clearly (e.g., clear glass, pure water, clear air).
* **Translucent materials:** Materials that allow light to pass through partially and diffusely, so that objects behind them cannot be seen clearly (e.g., frosted glass, oiled paper, tracing paper, thin cloth).
* **Opaque materials:** Materials that completely absorb or reflect light and do not allow any light to pass through them (e.g., wood, metals, thick cardboard, stone).

[END_PAGE]

[PDF_PAGE 168]
[PRINTED_PAGE 162]

[RUNNING_HEADER]
Light

[HEADING level=2 role=section]
Rectilinear propagation of light

[PARAGRAPH]
Rectilinear propagation of light is the property of light traveling in straight lines through a uniform, transparent medium.

[BEGIN ACTIVITY label="Activity 9.1"]
[INSTRUCTION]
Demonstrating that light travels in straight lines using the three-cardboard experiment.

[LIST style=numbered]
1. Take three identical cardboard sheets and punch a small hole in the exact center of each sheet.
2. Mount the cardboards vertically on stands aligned in a straight line on a table.
3. Place a lighted candle behind the first cardboard sheet.
4. Look through the hole in the third cardboard sheet.

[PARAGRAPH]
Observation: The candle flame is clearly visible only when all three holes are precisely aligned in a straight line. If any cardboard sheet is displaced slightly sideways, the flame disappears immediately, proving that light travels in straight lines.
[END ACTIVITY]

[HEADING level=3 role=subsection]
Rays and beams of light

[PARAGRAPH]
A **ray of light** is a line drawn in the direction in which light travels, represented in diagrams by a straight line with an arrowhead.

[PARAGRAPH]
A **beam of light** is a collection or bundle of parallel, converging, or diverging light rays:

[LIST style=bullet]
* **Parallel beam:** Light rays that travel parallel to each other without meeting (e.g., sunlight reaching Earth).
* **Converging beam:** Light rays that approach each other and meet at a single point.
* **Diverging beam:** Light rays that spread out from a common point source.

[END_PAGE]

[PDF_PAGE 169]
[PRINTED_PAGE 163]

[RUNNING_HEADER]
Light

[HEADING level=3 role=subsection]
Shadows and eclipses

[PARAGRAPH]
A shadow is a dark region formed behind an opaque object when it blocks light rays from a light source, direct consequence of rectilinear propagation of light.

[HEADING level=4 role=other]
1. Umbra and Penumbra

[LIST style=bullet]
* **Umbra:** The central, completely dark region of a shadow where all light rays from the source are blocked by the opaque object.
* **Penumbra:** The partial shadow surrounding the umbra where light from only a portion of an extended light source is blocked.

[HEADING level=4 role=other]
2. Eclipses

[PARAGRAPH]
An eclipse occurs when a celestial body passes into the shadow of another celestial body.

[LIST style=bullet]
* **Solar eclipse (Eclipse of the Sun):** Occurs when the Moon passes directly between the Earth and the Sun during a new moon, casting its shadow on the Earth.
* **Lunar eclipse (Eclipse of the Moon):** Occurs when the Earth passes directly between the Sun and the Moon during a full moon, casting its shadow over the Moon.

[HEADING level=3 role=subsection]
Pinhole camera

[PARAGRAPH]
A pinhole camera is a simple light-proof box with a tiny aperture (pinhole) on one side and a translucent screen (tracing paper) on the opposite side. It operates on the principle of rectilinear propagation of light to produce a real, inverted, and diminished image of an illuminated object.

[PARAGRAPH]
The magnification (\(m\)) of a pinhole camera is defined as the ratio of image height (\(h_i\)) to object height (\(h_o\)), which equals the ratio of image distance (\(v\)) to object distance (\(u\)):

[EQUATION]
m = \frac{h_i}{h_o} = \frac{v}{u}
[END_PAGE]

[PDF_PAGE 170]
[PRINTED_PAGE 164]

[RUNNING_HEADER]
Light

[BEGIN EXAMPLE label="Example 9.1"]
[PARAGRAPH]
A pinhole camera of length \(20\,\mathrm{cm}\) is placed at a distance of \(5\,\mathrm{m}\) from a tree of height \(10\,\mathrm{m}\). Calculate:
[QUESTION label="(a)"]
The magnification produced by the camera.
[QUESTION label="(b)"]
The height of the image formed on the screen.

[SOLUTION]
Given:
Camera length (image distance), \(v = 20\,\mathrm{cm} = 0.2\,\mathrm{m}\)
Object distance, \(u = 5\,\mathrm{m}\)
Object height, \(h_o = 10\,\mathrm{m}\)

(a) Magnification, \(m\):
[EQUATION]
m = \frac{v}{u} = \frac{0.2\,\mathrm{m}}{5\,\mathrm{m}} = 0.04

[PARAGRAPH]
(b) Image height, \(h_i\):

[EQUATION]
m = \frac{h_i}{h_o} \implies h_i = m \times h_o = 0.04 \times 10\,\mathrm{m} = 0.4\,\mathrm{m} = 40\,\mathrm{cm}

[PARAGRAPH]
The image height formed on the screen is \(40\,\mathrm{cm}\).

[END EXAMPLE]

[HEADING level=2 role=section]
Reflection of light

[PARAGRAPH]
Reflection of light is the bouncing back of light rays into the same medium when they strike a boundary or polished surface.

[HEADING level=3 role=subsection]
Types of reflection

[LIST style=bullet]
* **Regular (Specular) reflection:** Occurs when parallel rays of light strike a smooth, polished surface (like a plane mirror or calm water) and reflect parallel to each other in a definite direction.
* **Diffuse (Irregular) reflection:** Occurs when parallel rays strike a rough or uneven surface (like paper, wood, or stone) and reflect in all directions, making non-luminous objects visible.

[END_PAGE]

[PDF_PAGE 171]
[PRINTED_PAGE 165]

[RUNNING_HEADER]
Light

[HEADING level=3 role=subsection]
Laws of reflection

[PARAGRAPH]
When light is reflected from a plane surface, it obeys two fundamental laws of reflection:

[BEGIN NOTE kind=definition]
1. **First Law:** The incident ray, the reflected ray, and the normal at the point of incidence all lie in the same plane.
2. **Second Law:** The angle of incidence (\(i\)) is equal to the angle of reflection (\(r\)):
[EQUATION]
i = r
[END NOTE]

[PARAGRAPH]
Where:
- **Normal:** An imaginary line perpendicular to the reflecting surface at the point of incidence.
- **Angle of incidence (\(i\)):** The angle between the incident ray and the normal.
- **Angle of reflection (\(r\)):** The angle between the reflected ray and the normal.

[BEGIN ACTIVITY label="Activity 9.2"]
[INSTRUCTION]
Verifying the laws of reflection using a plane mirror, optical pins, and a drawing board.

[LIST style=numbered]
1. Fix a sheet of white paper on a drawing board and draw a straight line \(NM\) representing a plane mirror line.
2. Draw a normal line \(ON\) perpendicular to \(NM\) at point \(O\).
3. Draw an incident ray line making a known angle \(i = 30^\circ\) with the normal.
4. Place two pins \(P_1\) and \(P_2\) vertically along the incident ray line.
5. Look into the mirror and place two more pins \(P_3\) and \(P_4\) such that they align exactly in a straight line with the images of \(P_1\) and \(P_2\).
6. Remove the pins, join the positions of \(P_3\) and \(P_4\) to point \(O\), and measure the angle of reflection \(r\).

[PARAGRAPH]
Observation: The measured angle of reflection \(r\) is found to equal \(30^\circ\), verifying that \(i = r\).
[END ACTIVITY]

[END_PAGE]

[PDF_PAGE 172]
[PRINTED_PAGE 166]

[RUNNING_HEADER]
Light

[HEADING level=3 role=subsection]
Image formation in a plane mirror

[PARAGRAPH]
When an object is placed in front of a plane mirror, an image is formed behind the mirror by the geometrical intersection of reflected rays extended backwards.

[HEADING level=4 role=other]
Characteristics of images formed by a plane mirror

[LIST style=bullet]
* **Virtual:** Cannot be formed on a screen because reflected rays do not actually meet.
* **Upright (Erect):** Right-side up, oriented the same way as the object.
* **Same size:** Image height equals object height (magnification \(m = 1\)).
* **Same distance:** Distance of image behind mirror equals distance of object in front of mirror (\(v = u\)).
* **Laterally inverted:** Left and right sides are reversed.

[HEADING level=3 role=subsection]
Multiple reflections in inclined mirrors

[PARAGRAPH]
When two plane mirrors are inclined at an angle \(\theta\) to each other, multiple images are formed due to repeated reflections between the mirror surfaces. The total number of images (\(N\)) formed is given by:

[EQUATION]
N = \frac{360^\circ}{\theta} - 1
[PARAGRAPH]
When \(\frac{360^\circ}{\theta}\) is an odd integer, the formula holds when the object is placed symmetrically on the angle bisector. When two plane mirrors are parallel (\(\theta = 0^\circ\)), an infinite number of images (\(N = \infty\)) are formed.

[BEGIN EXAMPLE label="Example 9.2"]
[PARAGRAPH]
Calculate the number of images formed when an object is placed between two plane mirrors inclined at an angle of \(60^\circ\) to each other.

[SOLUTION]
Given: Angle of inclination, \(\theta = 60^\circ\)

[EQUATION]
N = \frac{360^\circ}{\theta} - 1 = \frac{360^\circ}{60^\circ} - 1 = 6 - 1 = 5

[PARAGRAPH]
The number of images formed is \(5\).

[END EXAMPLE]

[END_PAGE]

[PDF_PAGE 173]
[PRINTED_PAGE 167]

[RUNNING_HEADER]
Light

[HEADING level=3 role=subsection]
Applications of plane mirrors

[LIST style=bullet]
* **Dressing mirrors:** Used in homes and salons to view personal appearance.
* **Periscope:** An optical instrument consisting of a tube containing two plane mirrors inclined at \(45^\circ\) to each other, allowing an observer to view objects over obstacles or from submerged submarines.
* **Kaleidoscope:** A toy tube containing three plane mirrors arranged at \(60^\circ\) to produce beautiful symmetrical pattern reflections from loose beads.
* **Solar cookers:** Plane mirrors reflect and concentrate sunlight onto cooking vessels.

[BEGIN SUMMARY]
[HEADING level=3 role=other]
Summary of Chapter Nine
[LIST style=bullet]
* Light is a form of radiant energy that travels as transverse electromagnetic waves in straight lines (rectilinear propagation) at \(3.0 \times 10^8\,\mathrm{m\,s^{-1}}\).
* Materials are classified as transparent, translucent, or opaque depending on how they transmit light.
* Rectilinear propagation explains shadow formation (umbra and penumbra), solar and lunar eclipses, and the operation of pinhole cameras.
* Magnification of a pinhole camera is \(m = \frac{h_i}{h_o} = \frac{v}{u}\).
* Reflection obeys two laws: (1) incident ray, reflected ray, and normal lie in the same plane, and (2) angle of incidence equals angle of reflection (\(i = r\)).
* Plane mirror images are virtual, upright, same size, same distance behind the mirror, and laterally inverted.
* Two mirrors inclined at angle \(\theta\) form \(N = \frac{360^\circ}{\theta} - 1\) images.
[END SUMMARY]

[END_PAGE]

[PDF_PAGE 174]
[PRINTED_PAGE 168]

[RUNNING_HEADER]
Light
