[HEADING level=1 role=chapter]
Chapter Ten: Magnetism

[HEADING level=2 role=section]
Concept of magnetism and magnets

[PARAGRAPH]
Magnetism is a fundamental property of matter characterized by the force of attraction or repulsion exerted between magnetic materials and magnets. A magnet is a material or object that produces a magnetic field capable of attracting ferromagnetic substances such as iron, nickel, cobalt, and steel.

[HEADING level=3 role=subsection]
Magnetic and non-magnetic materials

[LIST style=bullet]
* **Magnetic (Ferromagnetic) materials:** Materials that are strongly attracted by a magnet and can be magnetized (e.g., iron, steel, nickel, cobalt, alnico).
* **Non-magnetic materials:** Materials that are not attracted by a magnet and cannot be magnetized (e.g., copper, aluminium, wood, plastic, glass, rubber, brass).

[HEADING level=3 role=subsection]
Poles of a magnet

[PARAGRAPH]
Every magnet possesses two regions of concentrated magnetic strength called magnetic poles:
- **North-seeking pole (North pole, N):** The pole that points towards the Earth's geographic North pole when freely suspended.
- **South-seeking pole (South pole, S):** The pole that points towards the Earth's geographic South pole when freely suspended.

[BEGIN NOTE kind=definition]
**Fundamental Law of Magnetism:** Like magnetic poles repel each other, while unlike magnetic poles attract each other (North repels North, South repels South, but North attracts South).
[END NOTE]

[END_PAGE]

[PDF_PAGE 176]
[PRINTED_PAGE 170]

[RUNNING_HEADER]
Magnetism

[BEGIN ACTIVITY label="Activity 10.1"]
[INSTRUCTION]
Testing the law of magnetism using bar magnets and a thread stand.

[LIST style=numbered]
1. Suspend a bar magnet horizontally using a piece of thread tied at its centre of gravity.
2. Allow the suspended magnet to come to rest in the magnetic North-South direction.
3. Bring the North pole of a second bar magnet close to the North pole of the suspended magnet.
4. Next, bring the South pole of the second magnet close to the North pole of the suspended magnet.

[PARAGRAPH]
Observation: When like poles (N-N) approach, the suspended magnet moves away (repulsion). When unlike poles (N-S) approach, the suspended magnet is drawn closer (attraction).
Conclusion: Like poles repel; unlike poles attract. Repulsion is the only sure test for magnetisation.
[END ACTIVITY]

[HEADING level=2 role=section]
Magnetisation and demagnetisation

[HEADING level=3 role=subsection]
Domain theory of magnetism

[PARAGRAPH]
According to the domain theory, ferromagnetic materials consist of tiny atomic regions called magnetic domains. Each domain acts like a microscopic bar magnet with its own North and South poles.

[LIST style=bullet]
* **Unmagnetised material:** The magnetic domains are randomly oriented, so their individual magnetic fields cancel out, leaving no net external magnetism.
* **Magnetised material:** External magnetic forces align the domains so that their magnetic fields point in the same parallel direction, creating macroscopically observable North and South poles at opposite ends.

[END_PAGE]

[PDF_PAGE 177]
[PRINTED_PAGE 171]

[RUNNING_HEADER]
Magnetism

[HEADING level=3 role=subsection]
Methods of magnetisation

[PARAGRAPH]
Unmagnetised ferromagnetic materials (like steel bars) can be converted into magnets using several methods:

[LIST style=bullet]
* **Single touch method:** Stroking a steel bar repeatedly from one end to the other in one direction with the same pole of a permanent bar magnet.
* **Double (Divided) touch method:** Stroking a steel bar from the centre outwards to opposite ends simultaneously using opposite poles of two bar magnets.
* **Electrical method (Solenoid):** Placing a steel bar inside a cylindrical coil of insulated copper wire (solenoid) connected to a direct current (DC) source. The magnetic pole produced at an end depends on the direction of current flow (Clockwise = South pole, Anticlockwise = North pole).
* **Induction method:** Placing a magnetic material near or in contact with a strong magnet without stroking.

[HEADING level=3 role=subsection]
Methods of demagnetisation

[PARAGRAPH]
Demagnetisation is the process of scrambling aligned magnetic domains back into random orientations, destroying external magnetism:

[LIST style=bullet]
* **Heating:** Heating a magnet to redness disrupts domain alignment through thermal agitation.
* **Hammering or rough handling:** Striking a magnet repeatedly with a hammer oriented East-West weakens domain alignment.
* **Electrical method (AC Solenoid):** Placing the magnet inside a solenoid connected to an alternating current (AC) supply oriented East-West and slowly withdrawing it to a distance.

[END_PAGE]

[PDF_PAGE 178]
[PRINTED_PAGE 172]

[RUNNING_HEADER]
Magnetism

[HEADING level=2 role=section]
Magnetic fields

[PARAGRAPH]
A magnetic field is the region around a magnet or current-carrying conductor within which magnetic forces can be detected by magnetic materials or a plotting compass.

[HEADING level=3 role=subsection]
Magnetic field lines and patterns

[PARAGRAPH]
Magnetic field lines (lines of force) are imaginary lines used to represent the direction and strength of a magnetic field:

[LIST style=bullet]
* Magnetic field lines emerge from the **North pole** and enter the **South pole** externally.
* Field lines never cross or intersect each other.
* Closer field lines indicate a stronger magnetic field.
* A neutral point is a point in a magnetic field where the resultant magnetic field strength is zero due to equal and opposite cancelling magnetic fields.

[BEGIN ACTIVITY label="Activity 10.2"]
[INSTRUCTION]
Mapping magnetic field lines around a bar magnet using iron filings.

[LIST style=numbered]
1. Place a bar magnet beneath a sheet of stiff white cardboard.
2. Sprinkle iron filings evenly over the cardboard surface.
3. Gently tap the edge of the cardboard sheet repeatedly.

[PARAGRAPH]
Observation: The iron filings align themselves along curved continuous lines stretching from the North pole to the South pole, revealing the field line pattern.
[END ACTIVITY]

[END_PAGE]

[PDF_PAGE 179]
[PRINTED_PAGE 173]

[RUNNING_HEADER]
Magnetism

[HEADING level=2 role=section]
Earth's magnetic field

[PARAGRAPH]
The Earth acts like a giant spherical magnet with a magnetic field surrounding it. The Earth's magnetic South pole is situated near the geographical North pole, and the Earth's magnetic North pole is near the geographical South pole. This is why the North pole of a freely suspended compass needle points towards the Earth's geographical North direction.

[HEADING level=3 role=subsection]
Magnetic declination and dip

[LIST style=bullet]
* **Magnetic declination (Angle of variation):** The angle between the geographic meridian (true north) and the magnetic meridian (magnetic north) at a given place on Earth.
* **Angle of dip (Magnetic inclination):** The angle between the horizontal plane and the direction of the Earth's total magnetic field vector at a specific location, measured using a dip needle.

[HEADING level=3 role=subsection]
Applications of magnets

[LIST style=bullet]
* **Magnetic compasses:** Used in navigation by ships, aircraft, and hikers.
* **Electric motors and generators:** Utilize powerful magnetic fields to convert between electrical and mechanical energy.
* **Loudspeakers and microphones:** Convert electrical audio signals into mechanical sound vibrations.
* **Magnetic storage media:** Used in credit card magnetic strips, hard disk drives, and magnetic tapes.
* **Electromagnets in cranes:** Used in scrap yards to lift heavy iron and steel objects.

[END_PAGE]

[PDF_PAGE 180]
[PRINTED_PAGE 174]

[RUNNING_HEADER]
Magnetism

[BEGIN SUMMARY]
[HEADING level=3 role=other]
Summary of Chapter Ten
[LIST style=bullet]
* Magnetism is the property of attracting ferromagnetic materials (iron, steel, nickel, cobalt).
* Magnets have two poles: North (N) and South (S). Like poles repel; unlike poles attract. Repulsion is the test for magnetisation.
* Domain theory states that ferromagnetic substances consist of tiny atomic magnetic domains.
* Magnetisation methods include stroking (single/double touch), electrical DC solenoid, and induction.
* Demagnetisation methods include heating to redness, hammering in an East-West direction, and using an AC solenoid.
* Magnetic field lines run from N to S outside the magnet and never cross.
* The Earth behaves as a giant magnet, possessing magnetic declination and dip angle properties.
[END SUMMARY]

[HEADING level=1 role=chapter]
Chapter Eleven: Introduction to Electricity

[HEADING level=2 role=section]
Electrostatics

[PARAGRAPH]
Electrostatics is the branch of physics that deals with static electric charges at rest, their forces, and their interactions.

[HEADING level=3 role=subsection]
Types of electric charge

[PARAGRAPH]
There are two fundamental types of electric charge in nature:
- **Positive charge (+):** Acquired by glass rubbed with silk (due to loss of electrons).
- **Negative charge (-):** Acquired by ebonite or polythene rubbed with fur or wool (due to gain of electrons).

[BEGIN NOTE kind=definition]
**Fundamental Law of Electrostatics:** Like electric charges repel each other, while unlike electric charges attract each other.
[END NOTE]

[HEADING level=3 role=subsection]
Methods of charging bodies

[LIST style=bullet]
* **Charging by friction (rubbing):** Transfer of electrons between two insulating bodies when rubbed together.
* **Charging by contact (conduction):** Transfer of electric charge from a charged body to a neutral conductor by direct physical contact.
* **Charging by induction:** Redistribution of charges on a neutral conductor brought close to a charged body without physical contact, followed by earthing.

[END_PAGE]

[PDF_PAGE 182]
[PRINTED_PAGE 176]

[RUNNING_HEADER]
Introduction to Electricity

[HEADING level=3 role=subsection]
Gold-leaf electroscope

[PARAGRAPH]
A gold-leaf electroscope is an instrument used to detect the presence, sign, and magnitude of electric charges on a body.

[PARAGRAPH]
It consists of a vertical metal rod with a brass disc at the top and a thin, flexible gold leaf (or aluminium leaf) attached to its lower end alongside a fixed metal plate, enclosed inside a glass-windowed earthed metal box to protect it from air currents.

[LIST style=bullet]
* **Detecting charge:** When a charged object touches or approaches the disc, charge spreads to the leaf and fixed plate. Since they carry like charges, mutual repulsion causes the leaf to diverge (deflect).
* **Testing sign of charge:** Bring an unknown charge near a positively charged electroscope disc. An increase in leaf divergence indicates positive charge; a decrease indicates negative charge.

[HEADING level=3 role=subsection]
Electric field and conductors

[PARAGRAPH]
An electric field is a region surrounding an electric charge in which an electric force is exerted on another charged particle.

[LIST style=bullet]
* **Conductors:** Materials that contain free mobile electrons allowing electric charge to flow easily (e.g., metals like copper, aluminium, silver, graphite, salt solutions).
* **Insulators:** Materials that do not contain free mobile electrons, preventing electric charge from flowing through them (e.g., rubber, plastic, dry wood, glass, ebonite).

[END_PAGE]

[PDF_PAGE 183]
[PRINTED_PAGE 177]

[RUNNING_HEADER]
Introduction to Electricity

[HEADING level=2 role=section]
Current electricity

[PARAGRAPH]
Current electricity is the flow of electric charges (electrons) through a conducting path or closed electric circuit.

[HEADING level=3 role=subsection]
Electric current

[PARAGRAPH]
Electric current (\(I\)) is defined as the rate of flow of electric charge (\(Q\)) through a conductor:

[EQUATION]
I = \frac{Q}{t} \implies Q = I \times t
[PARAGRAPH]
Where:
- \(I\) is current in Amperes (\(\mathrm{A}\))
- \(Q\) is electric charge in Coulombs (\(\mathrm{C}\))
- \(t\) is time in seconds (\(\mathrm{s}\))

[BEGIN NOTE kind=definition]
The SI unit of electric current is the **Ampere** (\(\mathrm{A}\)). One Ampere is the current flowing when one Coulomb of charge passes through a conductor cross-section per second (\(1\,\mathrm{A} = 1\,\mathrm{C\,s^{-1}}\)).
[END NOTE]

[BEGIN EXAMPLE label="Example 11.1"]
[PARAGRAPH]
Calculate the electric charge that passes through a lamp bulb filament carrying a steady current of \(0.5\,\mathrm{A}\) for a period of \(4\,\mathrm{minutes}\).

[SOLUTION]
Given:
Current, \(I = 0.5\,\mathrm{A}\)
Time, \(t = 4\,\mathrm{minutes} = 4 \times 60\,\mathrm{s} = 240\,\mathrm{s}\)

Charge, \(Q\):
[EQUATION]
Q = I \times t = 0.5\,\mathrm{A} \times 240\,\mathrm{s} = 120\,\mathrm{C}

[PARAGRAPH]
The electric charge passing through the bulb is \(120\,\mathrm{Coulombs}\).

[END EXAMPLE]

[END_PAGE]

[PDF_PAGE 184]
[PRINTED_PAGE 178]

[RUNNING_HEADER]
Introduction to Electricity

[HEADING level=3 role=subsection]
Electromotive force (e.m.f) and Potential Difference (p.d)

[PARAGRAPH]
- **Electromotive force (e.m.f):** The total electrical energy supplied per unit charge by an electric source (battery/cell) in driving charge around a complete circuit including the cell itself.
- **Potential difference (p.d):** The electrical energy converted into other forms (heat, light) per unit charge passing between two points in a circuit.

[PARAGRAPH]
Both e.m.f and potential difference are measured in **Volts** (\(\mathrm{V}\)) using a voltmeter connected in parallel across the component. One Volt is defined as one Joule of energy converted per Coulomb of charge (\(1\,\mathrm{V} = 1\,\mathrm{J\,C^{-1}}\)).

[HEADING level=3 role=subsection]
Simple electric circuits and components

[PARAGRAPH]
An electric circuit is a continuous conducting path containing a power source, connecting wires, switches, loads, and measuring instruments.

[LIST style=bullet]
* **Ammeter:** Instrument used to measure electric current; connected in **series** in a circuit (has very low resistance).
* **Voltmeter:** Instrument used to measure potential difference; connected in **parallel** across a component (has very high resistance).
* **Switch:** Device used to open or close an electric circuit.
* **Resistor:** Component that opposes the flow of electric current.

[END_PAGE]

[PDF_PAGE 185]
[PRINTED_PAGE 179]

[RUNNING_HEADER]
Introduction to Electricity

[HEADING level=3 role=subsection]
Simple primary cells and secondary cells

[LIST style=bullet]
* **Primary cells:** Electrochemical cells that convert chemical energy into electrical energy through irreversible chemical reactions; cannot be recharged (e.g., Simple cell, Leclanché cell, Dry cell/Zinc-carbon cell).
* **Secondary cells:** Electrochemical cells whose chemical reactions are reversible by passing electrical current backwards through them; can be recharged repeatedly (e.g., Lead-acid accumulator, Lithium-ion battery, Nickel-cadmium battery).

[HEADING level=4 role=other]
Defects of a Simple Cell

[LIST style=bullet]
* **Polarisation:** Accumulation of hydrogen gas bubbles around the copper anode, creating an opposing e.m.f and increasing internal resistance. Remedied by adding a depolariser such as manganese(IV) oxide (\(\ce{MnO2}\)) or potassium dichromate.
* **Local action:** Wastage of the zinc cathode plate due to impurities (like iron) creating microscopic local currents. Remedied by amalgamating zinc with mercury or using pure zinc.

[END_PAGE]

[PDF_PAGE 186]
[PRINTED_PAGE 180]

[RUNNING_HEADER]
Introduction to Electricity

[BEGIN SUMMARY]
[HEADING level=3 role=other]
Summary of Chapter Eleven
[LIST style=bullet]
* Electrostatics deals with static electric charges (+ and -). Like charges repel; unlike charges attract.
* Charging methods include friction, contact, and induction.
* A gold-leaf electroscope detects electric charges and determines their sign.
* Electric current is the rate of flow of charge: \(I = \frac{Q}{t}\), measured in Amperes (\(\mathrm{A}\)).
* Electromotive force (e.m.f) and potential difference (p.d) are measured in Volts (\(\mathrm{V}\)) using a voltmeter in parallel.
* Ammeters measure current and are connected in series.
* Primary cells cannot be recharged (suffer from polarisation and local action); secondary cells are rechargeable accumulators.
[END SUMMARY]

Glossary

[HEADING level=2 role=section]
Glossary of Key Physics Terms

[DEFINITION_LIST]
Adhesion	Force of attraction between molecules of different substances.
Buoyancy	Upward force exerted by a fluid on an object immersed in it.
Cohesion	Force of attraction between molecules of the same substance.
Density	Mass per unit volume of a substance (\(\rho = \frac{m}{V}\)).
Elasticity	Property of a material to regain its original shape and size after deforming forces are removed.

Inertia	Resistance of a body to any change in its state of rest or uniform motion.
Luminous	Body that generates and emits its own light.
Pascal's Principle	Transmission of pressure undiminished in enclosed fluids.
Vector	Physical quantity possessing both magnitude and direction.

[PDF_PAGE 201]

[RUNNING_HEADER]
Work, energy and power

[PARAGRAPH]
These conditions are illustrated in Figure 9.3.

[CAPTION label="Figure 9.3"]
Figure 9.3: Illustration of the conditions for the work to be done

[LIST style=lettered]
(a) If an object is lifted or lowered shown in Figure 9.3 (a), a vertical force is exerted on the object and it moves parallel to the force so work is done.
(b) If an object is simply held in the air shown in Figure 9.3 (b), an upward force is exerted on the object but the object is not moving. Thus, no work is done.
(c) If an object is held while walking across the room as displayed in Figure 9.3 (c), an upward force is exerted on it and the object is moving. However, the movement is not parallel to the force, no work is done.
(d) If the force and distance moved by the object are in opposite directions, work is done by the force.
(e) If the force and distance moved are at some angle, work is done only by the component of the force that is parallel to the distance moved.
(f) If the force and distance moved are perpendicular (at a right angle) to each other, no work is done.

[EXAMPLE label="Example 9.1"]
A force of 80 N pulls a box along a smooth and level ground through a distance of 5 m. Calculate the work done by the force.

[SOLUTION]
Force \(F = 80\,\mathrm{N}\), distance \(d = 5\,\mathrm{m}\), work done (\(W\)) is given as:

[EQUATION]
W = F 	imes d
[EQUATION]
W = 80\,\mathrm{N} 	imes 5\,\mathrm{m} = 400\,\mathrm{J}
[PARAGRAPH]
Therefore, the work done is 400 J.

[END_PAGE]

[PDF_PAGE 202]
[PRINTED_PAGE 196]

[RUNNING_HEADER]
Work, energy and power

[EXAMPLE label="Example 9.2"]
How much work is done in lifting a 7 kg object through a height of 2 m and then holding it at that height for 10 s? (Assume, \(g = 10\,\mathrm{N\,kg^{-1}}\))

[SOLUTION]
Mass \(m = 7\,\mathrm{kg}\), distance \(d = 2\,\mathrm{m}\), \(g = 10\,\mathrm{N\,kg^{-1}}\).

[PARAGRAPH]
To lift an object or hold it in the air, an upward force equal to its weight must be exerted.

[PARAGRAPH]
Weight (N) of an object = mass (kg) of an object \(	imes\) acceleration due to gravity (\(\mathrm{N\,kg^{-1}}\))

[EQUATION]
w = mg
[PARAGRAPH]
But, \(\mathrm{weight} = \mathrm{Force}\). Then,

[EQUATION]
\mathrm{Force} = mg = 7\,\mathrm{kg} 	imes 10\,\mathrm{N\,kg^{-1}} = 70\,\mathrm{N}
[PARAGRAPH]
So, you must exert on the object an upward force of 70 N. To lift the object a distance of 2 m, the work done is calculated as follows:

[EQUATION]
W = F 	imes d = 70\,\mathrm{N} 	imes 2\,\mathrm{m} = 140\,\mathrm{J}
[PARAGRAPH]
To hold the object for 10 s: \(F = 70\,\mathrm{N}\), \(d = 0\,\mathrm{m}\).

[EQUATION]
W = F 	imes d = 70\,\mathrm{N} 	imes 0\,\mathrm{m} = 0\,\mathrm{J}
[PARAGRAPH]
So, 140 J of work was required to lift the object at a height of 2 m, but no additional work was done in holding the object at that height for 10 s.

[BEGIN EXERCISE label="Exercise 9.1"]
[INSTRUCTION]
Answer all questions (where necessary use \(g = 10\,\mathrm{N\,kg^{-1}}\)).

[QUESTION label="1."]
If a man pushes a van with a force of 300 N for a distance of 10 m, how much work does he do?

[QUESTION label="2."]
A man lifts a load of 20 kg through a height of 3 m. Calculate the work done.

[QUESTION label="3."]
Zenge lifts a brick of mass 10 kg from the floor to a shelf that is 3 m high. How much work does she do?

[QUESTION label="4."]
In each of the following cases, state whether work is done or not.
(a) A cow pulling a 50 kg cart of maize along a rough road.
(b) A teacher holding a book in his hand while talking to his friend.
(c) An aeroplane flying upward at take-off.
(d) An aeroplane moving horizontally 3 000 m above sea level.

[QUESTION label="5."]
A sack of rice which weighs 900 N is lifted to a height of 2 m. Calculate the work done against gravity.
[END EXERCISE]

[END_PAGE]

[PDF_PAGE 203]
[PRINTED_PAGE 197]

[RUNNING_HEADER]
Work, energy and power

[HEADING level=2 role=section]
Concept of energy

[PARAGRAPH]
Energy plays a role in our daily activities, such as cooking, freezing, lighting, and manufacturing. In science and engineering, energy is defined as the ability to perform work. An object possessing energy can do work and transfer that energy to another object, enabling it to perform work as well. In essence, energy quantifies an object's capacity to do work.

[HEADING level=3 role=subsection]
Forms of energy

[PARAGRAPH]
Energy exists in different forms, including mechanical energy, chemical energy, electrical energy, heat energy, light energy, sound energy and nuclear energy. One form of energy can be converted into another form, as illustrated in Figure 9.5.

[CAPTION label="Figure 9.4"]
Figure 9.4: Driving a nail into wood

[CAPTION label="Figure 9.5"]
Figure 9.5: Forms of energy

[PARAGRAPH]
Mechanical energy is the energy possessed by an object due to its position or motion. The mechanical energy due to the position of the object is referred to as the potential energy (PE). Meanwhile, the mechanical energy due to the motion of the objects is known as kinetic energy (KE).

[END_PAGE]

[PDF_PAGE 204]
[PRINTED_PAGE 198]

[RUNNING_HEADER]
Work, energy and power

[PARAGRAPH]
Electrical energy is due to the kinetic energy of the moving electric charges in a circuit. Electric charges pose energy due to their position. This energy is known as the electric potential energy and is determined by the electric forces between the charges. Devices such as batteries store energy in form of the electric potential energy. That is, when a battery is charged it stores the electric potential energy so that it can be used later. Figure 9.6 shows some batteries connected to an electric bulb converting stored electrical energy into light.

[CAPTION label="Figure 9.6"]
Figure 9.6: Battery powering an electric bulb

[PARAGRAPH]
Heat or thermal energy is the energy that comes from the movement of particles within a substance. As particles move and collide, they generate heat, which is a measure of thermal energy. The faster the particles move, the higher the thermal energy and, consequently, the temperature of the substance. Thermal energy can be transferred between objects through conduction, convection, or radiation. It plays a crucial role in everyday processes, from cooking food to powering engines and regulating climate. Thermal energy is useful in cooking as shown in Figure 9.7.

[CAPTION label="Figure 9.7"]
Figure 9.7: Thermal energy in cooking

[CAPTION label="Figure 9.8"]
Figure 9.8: Nuclear reactor

[PARAGRAPH]
Electromagnetic energy (infrared radiation, visible radiation, ultraviolet radiation, and so on) is associated with accelerating electric charge, and therefore with the changing velocity of that charge or oscillating electric and magnetic fields. Radiant light energy is the most common form of electromagnetic energy. The sun provides radiant energy to the earth as shown in Figure 9.9.

[CAPTION label="Figure 9.9"]
Figure 9.9: Radiant energy from the sun

[END_PAGE]

[PDF_PAGE 205]
[PRINTED_PAGE 199]

[RUNNING_HEADER]
Work, energy and power

[PARAGRAPH]
Sound energy is also a form of energy that is transferred in the form of waves arising from the vibration of a membrane and mechanical oscillations. Sources of sound may be speakers, sound boxes, and vibrations from the wings of insects. A microphone converts sound energy into an electrical signal and a loudspeaker converts electrical energy into sound energy. Figure 9.10 shows an example of a loudspeaker.

[CAPTION label="Figure 9.10"]
Figure 9.10: A loudspeaker

[HEADING level=3 role=subsection]
Kinetic and potential energy

[PARAGRAPH]
Potential and kinetic energy are due to the position and motion of an object, respectively. In this section, you will learn about kinetic energy and potential energy.

[HEADING level=4 role=other]
Kinetic energy

[PARAGRAPH]
This is the energy possessed by an object due to its motion. Any matter that is moving has kinetic energy. Both a plane flying through the air and an atom vibrating in a solid have kinetic energy. Consider a 7-ton lorry and a 1-ton mini vehicle both travelling at a speed of \(80\,\mathrm{km\,h^{-1}}\). In case of an accident, one expects the lorry to cause more damage than the mini vehicle. This is because the lorry possesses higher kinetic energy than the mini vehicle. The kinetic energy of an object depends on its mass and speed. For a body of mass \(m\) travelling at speed \(v\), its kinetic energy (KE) is expressed as:

[EQUATION]
\mathrm{KE} = \frac{1}{2}mv^{2}
[PARAGRAPH]
The SI unit of kinetic energy is Joule (J) which is equivalent to \(\mathrm{kg\,m^{2}\,s^{-2}}\). When the velocity of a body increases or decreases, its kinetic energy changes. Consider an object of mass \(m\) initially moving with speed \(u\) and attains the final speed \(v\). The corresponding change in kinetic energy (\(\Delta\mathrm{KE}\)) is given as the difference between the object's final kinetic energy and initial kinetic energy. That is:

[EQUATION]
\Delta\mathrm{KE} = \mathrm{KE}_{\mathrm{final}} - \mathrm{KE}_{\mathrm{initial}} = \frac{1}{2}mv^{2} - \frac{1}{2}mu^{2} = \frac{1}{2}m(v^{2} - u^{2})
[END_PAGE]

[PDF_PAGE 206]
[PRINTED_PAGE 200]

[RUNNING_HEADER]
Work, energy and power

[EXAMPLE label="Example 9.3"]
An object has a mass of 5 kg. What is its kinetic energy if its speed is:
(a) \(5\,\mathrm{m\,s^{-1}}\)?
(b) \(10\,\mathrm{m\,s^{-1}}\)?

[SOLUTION]
Given that: mass (\(m\)) = 5 kg and speed (\(v\)) = \(5\,\mathrm{m\,s^{-1}}\) and \(10\,\mathrm{m\,s^{-1}}\).

(a) \(\mathrm{KE} = \frac{1}{2}mv^{2} = \frac{1}{2} 	imes 5\,\mathrm{kg} 	imes (5\,\mathrm{m\,s^{-1}})^{2} = 62.5\,\mathrm{J}\)

(b) \(\mathrm{KE} = \frac{1}{2}mv^{2} = \frac{1}{2} 	imes 5\,\mathrm{kg} 	imes (10\,\mathrm{m\,s^{-1}})^{2} = 250\,\mathrm{J}\)

[PARAGRAPH]
Comparing the two examples, it shows that, if the speed of an object is doubled, its kinetic energy increases by a factor of 4.

[EXAMPLE label="Example 9.4"]
What is the kinetic energy of a 12 g bullet travelling at the speed of \(320\,\mathrm{m\,s^{-1}}\)?

[SOLUTION]
Given that mass (\(m\)) = \(12\,\mathrm{g} = 0.012\,\mathrm{kg}\), speed (\(v\)) = \(320\,\mathrm{m\,s^{-1}}\).

[EQUATION]
\mathrm{KE} = \frac{1}{2}mv^{2} = \frac{1}{2} 	imes 0.012\,\mathrm{kg} 	imes (320\,\mathrm{m\,s^{-1}})^{2} = 614.4\,\mathrm{J}
[PARAGRAPH]
Therefore, the kinetic energy of the bullet is 614.4 J.

[BEGIN ACTIVITY label="Activity 9.1"]
[HEADING level=3 role=other]
Activity 9.1
[OBJECTIVES]
Aim: To investigate the relationship between work and kinetic energy.
[LIST style=bullet]
* Materials: Wooden block, PVC tube, metre rule and metal ball
[END ACTIVITY]

[CAPTION label="Figure 9.12"]
Figure 9.12: A walking elephant

[PARAGRAPH]
A walking elephant in Figure 9.12 has high kinetic energy because of its large mass. If one collides with a walking elephant, part of its kinetic energy can be transferred to the person, resulting in serious injuries.

[END_PAGE]

[PDF_PAGE 207]
[PRINTED_PAGE 201]

[RUNNING_HEADER]
Work, energy and power

[HEADING level=4 role=other]
Potential energy

[PARAGRAPH]
Potential energy is the energy possessed by an object due to its position. An object can have potential energy if it is acted upon by a restoring force that tends to restore it to its initial position after it has been displaced. For example, a body raised from the ground to a certain height has gravitational potential energy due to gravitational force. On release, it returns to the ground, thus its potential energy relative to the ground becomes zero. Another type of potential energy is due to elastic force in a compressed spring (Figure 9.13). This is elastic potential energy.

[CAPTION label="Figure 9.13"]
Figure 9.13: Compressing spring

[PARAGRAPH]
Several restoring forces lead to various types of potential energies. Two types of potential energy are briefly discussed here.

[HEADING level=5 role=other]
Elastic potential energy

[PARAGRAPH]
This is the energy stored in objects like springs as a result of reversible deformation. When a force is applied to a spring, it gets compressed or stretched. But when the force is removed, it regains its original shape and size. In the deformed state, the spring has elastic potential energy.

[CAPTION label="Figure 9.14"]
Figure 9.14: Force-extension graph

[PARAGRAPH]
According to Hooke's law: \(\mathrm{Force}\,(F) = \mathrm{spring\,constant}\,(k) 	imes \mathrm{extension}\,(x)\), \(F = kx\). The area under the force-extension graph represents the work done in stretching or compressing the elastic materials. The energy stored in the stretched spring is an elastic potential energy.

[EQUATION]
\mathrm{Area} = \frac{1}{2} 	imes \mathrm{base} 	imes \mathrm{height} = \frac{1}{2} 	imes x 	imes F = \frac{1}{2} 	imes x 	imes kx = \frac{1}{2}kx^{2}
[PARAGRAPH]
Thus, elastic potential energy stored in the elastic material when it extends by a distance \(x\) is given by:

[EQUATION]
\mathrm{PE} = \frac{1}{2}kx^{2}
[END_PAGE]

[PDF_PAGE 208]
[PRINTED_PAGE 202]

[RUNNING_HEADER]
Work, energy and power

[HEADING level=5 role=other]
Gravitational potential energy

[PARAGRAPH]
Suppose a force \(F\) is used to lift an object with a mass (\(m\)) from an initial height (\(h_i\)) to a final height (\(h_f\)) at a constant velocity, as described in Figure 9.15. Since the object moves in the direction of the force, work is done on the object.

[CAPTION label="Figure 9.15"]
Figure 9.15: An object is lifted from \(h_i\) to \(h_f\)

[PARAGRAPH]
The force required to lift an object at a constant velocity has a magnitude equal to the object's weight, \(mg\). \(F = mg\). Multiplying both sides by the displacement (\(d\)) gives: \(Fd = mgd\). The displacement of the object is equal to the change in its height: \(d = h_f - h_i\). Substituting this expression gives:

[EQUATION]
Fd = mg(h_f - h_i) = mgh_f - mgh_i
[PARAGRAPH]
The quantity on the left side is the work done. The difference between \(mgh_i\) and \(mgh_f\) is the change in potential energy: \(\Delta\mathrm{PE} = W\). If \(h_i = 0\), then:

[EQUATION]
\mathrm{PE} = mgh
[PARAGRAPH]
The SI unit of PE is Joules (J), equivalent to \(\mathrm{kg\,m^{2}\,s^{-2}}\).

[EXAMPLE label="Example 9.5"]
A stone of mass 2 kg is resting on top of a building 25 m high. Calculate its potential energy relative to the ground. (Take \(g = 10\,\mathrm{N\,kg^{-1}}\))

[SOLUTION]
\(m = 2\,\mathrm{kg}\), \(h = 25\,\mathrm{m}\), \(g = 10\,\mathrm{N\,kg^{-1}}\).
\(\mathrm{PE} = mgh = 2\,\mathrm{kg} 	imes 10\,\mathrm{N\,kg^{-1}} 	imes 25\,\mathrm{m} = 500\,\mathrm{J}\).

[END_PAGE]

[PDF_PAGE 209]
[PRINTED_PAGE 203]

[RUNNING_HEADER]
Work, energy and power

[EXAMPLE label="Example 9.6"]
A 2 kg object is at rest on a table 1.2 m above the floor. The ceiling in the room is 2.8 m above the floor as shown in Figure 9.16.
(a) What is the gravitational potential energy of the object relative to; (i) the table top? (ii) the floor?
(b) Find the work done in lifting the object from the top of the table to the ceiling.

[CAPTION label="Figure 9.16"]
Figure 9.16: Object on table below ceiling

[SOLUTION]
(a) (i) At the table top \(h = 0\), \(\mathrm{PE} = mgh = 2\,\mathrm{kg} 	imes 10\,\mathrm{m\,s^{-2}} 	imes 0\,\mathrm{m} = 0\,\mathrm{J}\).
(ii) Relative to the floor \(h = 1.2\,\mathrm{m}\), \(\mathrm{PE} = mgh = 2\,\mathrm{kg} 	imes 10\,\mathrm{m\,s^{-2}} 	imes 1.2\,\mathrm{m} = 24\,\mathrm{J}\).
(b) \(d = 2.8\,\mathrm{m} - 1.2\,\mathrm{m} = 1.6\,\mathrm{m}\). \(\Delta\mathrm{PE} = mgh = 2\,\mathrm{kg} 	imes 10\,\mathrm{m\,s^{-2}} 	imes 1.6\,\mathrm{m} = 32\,\mathrm{J}\).
Therefore, the work done = 32 J.

[EXAMPLE label="Example 9.7"]
Find the elastic potential energy stored in the spring with \(k = 50\,\mathrm{N\,m^{-1}}\) when the spring is compressed by 0.2 m.

[SOLUTION]
\(k = 50\,\mathrm{N\,m^{-1}}\), \(x = 0.2\,\mathrm{m}\).
\(\mathrm{PE} = \frac{1}{2}kx^{2} = \frac{1}{2} 	imes 50\,\mathrm{N\,m^{-1}} 	imes (0.2\,\mathrm{m})^{2} = 1\,\mathrm{J}\).

[EXAMPLE label="Example 9.8"]
Find the elastic potential energy stored in the spring with \(k = 100\,\mathrm{N\,m^{-1}}\) when the spring is compressed by 0.1 m.

[SOLUTION]
\(k = 100\,\mathrm{N\,m^{-1}}\), \(x = 0.1\,\mathrm{m}\).
\(\mathrm{PE} = \frac{1}{2}kx^{2} = \frac{1}{2} 	imes 100\,\mathrm{N\,m^{-1}} 	imes (0.1\,\mathrm{m})^{2} = 0.5\,\mathrm{J}\).

[END_PAGE]

[PDF_PAGE 210]
[PRINTED_PAGE 204]

[HEADING level=3 role=subsection]
Transformation of energy

[PARAGRAPH]
Energy provides a capacity to do work. Different types of work require different forms of energy. Therefore, energy can be transformed from one form to another. The transformation of energy is referred as energy conversion. Transformation of energy occurs everywhere and every second of the day. However, during the energy transformation, the total amount of energy remains constant.

[END_PAGE]

[PDF_PAGE 211]
[PRINTED_PAGE 205]

[RUNNING_HEADER]
Work, energy and power

[CAPTION label="Figure 9.17"]
Figure 9.17: Work and energy are interchangeable

[CAPTION label="Figure 9.18"]
Figure 9.18: Fuelling a car at a filling station

[CAPTION label="Figure 9.19"]
Figure 9.19: Train that uses diesel engine

[PARAGRAPH]
When a stone drops from a cliff of height, \(h\), its PE decreases while its KE increases as it falls. On hitting the ground, some dust may fly off (KE) and some sound may be heard (sound energy). The point of impact may also become warmer as a result of the impact. This shows that, PE has been converted into heat energy. The energy changes are as follows:

[PARAGRAPH]
\(\mathrm{PE} 
ightarrow \mathrm{KE} 
ightarrow \mathrm{KE} + \mathrm{sound\,energy} + \mathrm{heat}\).

[HEADING level=3 role=subsection]
Principle of conservation of energy

[PARAGRAPH]
Suppose a 3 kg object is dropped from a height of 5 m above the ground as in Figure 9.20. Ignoring the effects of air resistance, the object will fall to the ground with an acceleration due to gravity, \(g\).

[CAPTION label="Figure 9.20"]
Figure 9.20: Free-falling object energy conservation

[PARAGRAPH]
Choosing the ground as the reference level, the PE of the object when released is: \(\mathrm{PE} = mgH = 3\,\mathrm{kg} 	imes 10\,\mathrm{N\,kg^{-1}} 	imes 5\,\mathrm{m} = 150\,\mathrm{J}\). Since its initial velocity was zero, it had no KE when released, \(\mathrm{KE}_i = 0\). Upon reaching the ground, it had no PE (\(\mathrm{PE}_f = 0\)). It does however have kinetic energy: \(\mathrm{KE}_f = 150\,\mathrm{J}\).

[PARAGRAPH]
The principle of conservation of energy states that: "Energy is neither created nor destroyed but can be transformed from one form to another."

[END_PAGE]

[PDF_PAGE 212]
[PRINTED_PAGE 206]

[RUNNING_HEADER]
Work, energy and power

[CAPTION label="Figure 9.21"]
Figure 9.21: Conservation of mechanical energy

[PARAGRAPH]
At points A and D, where the pendulum reaches the highest points above the equilibrium point, the total energy is equal to PE. At Point C, the pendulum is at its lowest point. The total energy is equal to the KE. At points like B, between the highest and the lowest points, total energy is the sum of KE and PE.

[HEADING level=4 role=other]
Transducer

[PARAGRAPH]
A device used to convert one form of energy to another is called a transducer. Table 9.2 shows some transducers and the corresponding energy transformation.

[TABLE columns=2 header_rows=1 label="Table 9.2"]
Transducer	Energy conversion process
Bulb	Light energy / Electric energy / Heat energy
Generator	Mechanical energy \(
ightarrow\) Electrical energy
Motor	Electrical energy \(
ightarrow\) Mechanical energy
Solar panel	Solar energy \(
ightarrow\) Electrical energy
Battery	Chemical energy \(
ightarrow\) Electrical energy
Heater	Electrical energy \(
ightarrow\) Heat energy
Microphone	Sound energy \(
ightarrow\) Electrical energy

[EXAMPLE label="Example 9.9"]
At its highest point A in Figure 9.22, a pendulum of mass 0.8 kg is 1.2 m above the equilibrium point. What will be its velocity as it swings through its lowest point?

[SOLUTION]
Height \(h = 1.2\,\mathrm{m}\), Mass \(m = 0.8\,\mathrm{kg}\), \(g = 10\,\mathrm{N\,kg^{-1}}\).
Due to conservation of energy, maximum KE at lowest position = maximum PE at highest position.

[EQUATION]
\frac{1}{2}mv^{2} = mgh \implies v = \sqrt{2gh} = \sqrt{2 	imes 10\,\mathrm{m\,s^{-2}} 	imes 1.2\,\mathrm{m}} = 4.9\,\mathrm{m\,s^{-1}}
[PARAGRAPH]
Therefore, the velocity of the pendulum is \(4.9\,\mathrm{m\,s^{-1}}\).

[END_PAGE]

[PDF_PAGE 213]
[PRINTED_PAGE 207]

[RUNNING_HEADER]
Work, energy and power

[HEADING level=2 role=section]
Concept of power

[PARAGRAPH]
Power is the rate at which work is done or energy is transferred.

[EQUATION]
\mathrm{Power}\,(P) = \frac{\mathrm{Work\,done}\,(W)}{\mathrm{Time\,taken}\,(t)} = \frac{E}{t}
[PARAGRAPH]
The SI unit of power is the Watt (W), which is equivalent to Joules per second (\(\mathrm{J\,s^{-1}}\)).

[TABLE columns=2 header_rows=1 label="Table 9.3"]
Power, P	Alternative formula
In terms of potential energy	\(P = \frac{mgh}{t}\)
In terms of kinetic energy	\(P = \frac{mv^{2}}{2t}\)
In terms of force and velocity	\(P = Fv\)
In terms of momentum	\(P = mvg\)

[PARAGRAPH]
Other units of power: 1 kilowatt (kW) = 1 000 W; 1 Megawatt (MW) = 1 000 000 W; 1 horsepower (hp) = 746 W.

[EXAMPLE label="Example 9.10"]
A model of a car of mass 20 kg moves at a speed of 6 m/s for 10 s. Find the power developed by its engine: (a) in watts (b) in horsepower.

[SOLUTION]
(a) \(P = \frac{mv^{2}}{2t} = \frac{20\,\mathrm{kg} 	imes (6\,\mathrm{m\,s^{-1}})^{2}}{2 	imes 10\,\mathrm{s}} = 36\,\mathrm{W}\).
(b) \(P = \frac{36\,\mathrm{W}}{746\,\mathrm{W\,hp^{-1}}} = 0.048\,\mathrm{hp}\).

[END_PAGE]

[PDF_PAGE 214]
[PRINTED_PAGE 208]

[RUNNING_HEADER]
Work, energy and power

[CAPTION label="Figure 9.23"]
Figure 9.23: Walking upstairs

[CAPTION label="Figure 9.24"]
Figure 9.24: An elephant crossing a river

[EXAMPLE label="Example 9.11"]
How much power is required for a car of mass 1 000 kg to change speed from 10 m/s to 40 m/s in 8 s?

[SOLUTION]
\(\Delta\mathrm{KE} = \frac{1}{2}m(v^{2} - u^{2}) = \frac{1}{2} 	imes 1000\,\mathrm{kg} 	imes (40^{2} - 10^{2})\,\mathrm{m^{2}\,s^{-2}} = 500 	imes 1500 = 750\,000\,\mathrm{J}\).
\(\mathrm{Power} = \frac{\Delta\mathrm{KE}}{t} = \frac{750\,000\,\mathrm{J}}{8\,\mathrm{s}} = 93\,750\,\mathrm{W} = 93.75\,\mathrm{kW}\).

[CAPTION label="Figure 9.25"]
Figure 9.25: Running upstairs

[EXAMPLE label="Example 9.12"]
A man whose mass is 80 kg walks up a flight of 25 steps each 20 cm high in 10 seconds. Find the power developed by the man in kilowatts.

[SOLUTION]
\(m = 80\,\mathrm{kg}\), \(n = 25\), \(h = 20\,\mathrm{cm} = 0.2\,\mathrm{m}\), \(t = 10\,\mathrm{s}\).
\(P = \frac{nmgh}{t} = \frac{25 	imes 80\,\mathrm{kg} 	imes 10\,\mathrm{N\,kg^{-1}} 	imes 0.2\,\mathrm{m}}{10\,\mathrm{s}} = 400\,\mathrm{W} = 0.4\,\mathrm{kW}\).

[END_PAGE]

[PRINTED_PAGE 209]

[RUNNING_HEADER]
Work, energy and power

[HEADING level=2 role=section]
Chapter summary

[LIST style=numbered]
1. Work is done when a force acts on an object and the object moves in the direction of the force. \(W = Fd\).
2. Energy is the ability to do work and is measured in Joules.
3. Mechanical energy consists of kinetic and potential energy.
4. Kinetic energy is the energy an object has due to its motion: \(\mathrm{KE} = \frac{1}{2}mv^{2}\).
5. Potential energy is the energy stored in an object due to its position: \(\mathrm{PE} = mgh\).
6. Power is the rate at which work is done: \(P = \frac{W}{t}\).

[END_PAGE]

[QUESTION label="1."]
Choose the most correct answer.
(i) Work is said to be done when: (a) force acts upon a body, but the body does not move (b) force acts upon a body and it moves it in the direction of applied force (c) force acts upon a body but the body does not move in the direction of the force (d) force acts upon a body and the body moves in opposite to the direction of applied force.
(ii) A constant horizontal force F displaces a box by distance d over a rough horizontal surface. The normal force acting on the box does NO work on the box during the motion because it is: (a) equal to the applied force (b) perpendicular to the applied force (c) equal and opposite to the weight of the box (d) perpendicular to the displacement of the box.
(iii) The food consumed by a human being produces: (a) only kinetic energy (b) only heat energy (c) both heat energy and chemical energy (d) only chemical energy.
(iv) Work done and energy are related in that: (a) they are both a result of mechanical processes (b) they have the same unit in which they are measured (c) they all explain how fast a certain task is performed (d) all are typical examples of chemical energy.
(v) A student calculates his power in running up a flight of stairs. How does he calculate his power? (a) \(\mathrm{height} 	imes \mathrm{time} 	imes \mathrm{weight}\) (b) \(\frac{\mathrm{height} 	imes \mathrm{weight}}{\mathrm{time}}\) (c) \(\frac{\mathrm{time} 	imes \mathrm{weight}}{\mathrm{height}}\) (d) \(\frac{\mathrm{time}}{\mathrm{height} 	imes \mathrm{weight}}\).
(vi) Which athlete jumps the highest? (a) 600 N, 1320 J (b) 700 N, 1610 J (c) 800 N, 1760 J (d) 900 N, 1800 J.
(vii) Ignoring air resistance, which statement about the total energy of a dropped ball is correct? (a) It is the same at all points (b) It is greatest at point 1 (c) It is greatest at point 2 (d) It is greatest at point 3.
(viii) Which pair of quantities has the same unit? (a) speed and force (b) work done and energy (c) work done and power (d) potential energy and power.
(ix) Ignoring friction, how much work is done in moving a 500 kg car up a 10 m high hill? (a) \(50 	imes 10^{7}\,\mathrm{J}\) (b) \(5.0 	imes 10^{3}\,\mathrm{J}\) (c) \(1.0 	imes 10^{5}\,\mathrm{J}\) (d) \(1.0 	imes 10^{6}\,\mathrm{J}\).
(x) Which equation shows the relationship between power and work done? (a) \(\mathrm{power} = \frac{\mathrm{work\,done}}{\mathrm{time\,taken}}\) (b) \(\mathrm{power} = \frac{\mathrm{work\,done}}{\mathrm{distance\,moved}}\) (c) \(\mathrm{power} = \mathrm{work\,done} 	imes \mathrm{time\,taken}\) (d) \(\mathrm{power} = \mathrm{work\,done} 	imes \mathrm{velocity}\).

[QUESTION label="2."]
[MATCHING]
[COLUMN_A title="Column A"]

(iii) PE
(iv) Joule

E. Mechanical energy
F. 750 W
G. SI unit of power
H. \(\frac{1}{2}mv^{2}\)
I. SI unit of energy
J. 746 W
[END_MATCHING]

[HEADING level=3 role=other]
Section B

[QUESTION label="3."]
(a) Define the term work. (b) What happens to work done when force and displacement are in the same direction? (c) What happens when force and displacement are in opposite directions?

[QUESTION label="4."]
(a) Define the term power. (b) How is power measured? (c) Express 6 900 J/s in horsepower.

[QUESTION label="5."]
Explain why a rotating wonder wheel becomes hot after a sudden stop.

[QUESTION label="6."]
Calculate the work done in each case below. Indicate if the work is done on the object or by the object. (a) A 10 kg object is lifted to a height of 5 m above the ground. (b) The object is held at a height of 5 m for 7 s. (c) The object is lowered back to the ground.

[QUESTION label="7."]
A motor exerts a horizontal force of 200 N in pulling a box 10 m across a level floor. How much work does the motor do?

[QUESTION label="8."]
An object is dropped and falls to the ground. Is there any work done while it is falling? If so, what force is responsible? Is the work done on or by the object?

[QUESTION label="9."]
A 1 000 kg car is travelling down the road at a speed of 15 m/s. How much kinetic energy does it have?

[QUESTION label="10."]
A 1000 kg car travelling at 15 m/s has its speed increased to 45 m/s. How much kinetic energy would it have? How many times greater is the kinetic energy at 45 m/s than at 15 m/s?

[QUESTION label="11."]
Rock A has a mass of 2 kg and a speed of 1 m/s. Rock B has a mass of 1 kg and a speed of 2 m/s. Which rock has more kinetic energy?

[END_PAGE]

[PDF_PAGE 218]
[PRINTED_PAGE 212]

[BEGIN EXERCISE label="Revision exercise 9 (continued)"]

[RUNNING_HEADER]
Work, energy and power

[QUESTION label="12."]
If your mass is 45 kg, how much work would you do in climbing a ladder that is 5 m high?

[QUESTION label="13."]
The total mass of a wheelbarrow and its load is 90 kg. A worker pushes the wheelbarrow and loads up a plank of length 2.0 m onto a platform 0.65 m high. If the worker exerts a force of 300 N along the plank: (i) Calculate the gravitational potential energy gained. (ii) Calculate the work done by the worker. (iii) Suggest why the answers in (i) and (ii) are different.

[QUESTION label="14."]
(a) Discuss the energy changes in a firework. (b) A 3.00 kg object is lifted to a shelf 2.50 m above the floor. (i) What was the work done? (ii) What is the PE on the shelf? (iii) What will its velocity be when it hits the floor if it falls?

[QUESTION label="15."]
Which object has more kinetic energy between a 10 kg object travelling at 5 m/s and a 5 kg object travelling at 10 m/s?

[QUESTION label="16."]
How much work is required to stop a 1 500 kg car travelling at 20 m/s?

[QUESTION label="17."]
A 2 kg object is on a tabletop 1.1 m above the floor. How much work is required to lift it to a shelf 1.3 m above the floor?

[QUESTION label="18."]
A 0.5 kg ball is dropped from a height of 2.3 m above the floor. If upon hitting the floor it loses 3 J of energy, how high will it bounce?

[QUESTION label="19."]
A 3 kg box starts sliding down a 3.3 m high hill with initial velocity 5 m/s. If at the bottom its velocity is 7.5 m/s, how much work is done to overcome friction?

[QUESTION label="20."]
A student pulls a spring with \(k = 120\,\mathrm{N\,m^{-1}}\) stretching it from 0.10 m to 0.20 m. Determine the elastic potential energy stored in the spring.
[END EXERCISE]

[END_PAGE]

[HEADING level=1 role=chapter]
Glossary

[DEFINITION_LIST]
Acceleration	the rate of change of velocity of an object with time symbolised as a
Acceleration due to gravity	acceleration produced as a result of the attraction of the earth to an object
Accurate measurement	One that yields a result that is very close to the actual value
Action-at-a-distance	exist between two interacting objects which are not in physical contact with each other yet they exert a push or pull upon each other
Actual weight	the weight of a body in the air
Adhesion	action of attraction between molecules of different substances
Adhesive force	the force of attraction that exists between molecules of different substances
Ammeter	a device for measuring electric current in a circuit
Aneroid barometer	an instrument for measuring pressure. It contains no fluid
Apparatus	tools or equipment required to perform an activity in the laboratory
Apparent loss in weight	the difference in weight when a body is submerged in a fluid versus in air
Apparent weight	the weight of a body in a fluid
Applied force	the push or pull exerted on an object
Archimedes' principle	describes the relationship between upthrust acting on a body and the weight of liquid it displaces
Astronomy	the scientific study of the moon, sun, stars, and planets

[END_PAGE]

[PDF_PAGE 220]
[PRINTED_PAGE 214]

[DEFINITION_LIST]
Atmospheric pressure	the force or weight per unit area applied on all objects on the earth's surface due to air
Average velocity	the average rate at which displacement changes with time
Bicycle pump	a type of force pump designed for inflating bicycle tyres
Biology	the study of living organisms
Buoyancy	the ability of an object to float in a fluid
Buoyant force	the upward force that causes immersed objects to float or rise in a liquid
Burette	a glass tube with markings on the side and a tap at the bottom
Chemical energy	the energy obtained from food or fuel through chemical processes
Chemistry	the scientific study of the properties and behaviour of matter
Cohesion	the action of molecules of a substance to attract each other
Cohesive forces	molecular force exerted between molecules of the same kind
Compressional forces	force applied to an object resulting in a decrease in size
Contact force	force which exists when two interacting objects are physically touching
Density	mass of an object per unit volume
Digital balance	sensitive weighing balance measuring masses to an accuracy of 0.001 g or better

[END_PAGE]

[PDF_PAGE 221]
[PRINTED_PAGE 215]

[DEFINITION_LIST]
Derived quantities	obtained by multiplying or dividing two or more fundamental quantities
Derived unit	obtained by combining two or more fundamental units
Displacement	distance covered in a particular direction
Distance	length of the path taken by an object while in motion
Elastic energy	energy stored in objects like springs as a result of reversible deformation
Energy	ability to do work
Elasticity	ability of a deformed body to return to its original shape and size
Elastic limit	maximum point of deformation that a body can undergo reversibly
Floating	tendency of an object to be suspended in or remain on the surface of a fluid
Force	a push or a pull experienced by an object
Fluid	a substance such as a liquid or gas whose molecules flow freely
Force of gravity	the pull by which the earth attracts other objects towards it
Force pump	a pump that uses pressure to draw water to another surface
Free-fall	motion of objects falling to the ground without air resistance due to gravity
Frictional force	resistive force arising from two objects in contact moving relative to one another
Fundamental force	basic force in nature which cannot be explained by another force
Hydrometer	instrument used for determining the relative density of liquids

[END_PAGE]

[PDF_PAGE 222]
[PRINTED_PAGE 216]

[DEFINITION_LIST]
Instrumental error	an error that arises as a result of a defect or mishandling of an instrument
Law of floatation	relationship between a floating body and the weight of displaced fluid
Least count	difference between main scale division and vernier scale division
Length	distance or dimension between two points
Lift pump	pump used to raise underground water to the surface
Mass	quantity of matter in an object, measured using a beam balance
Mathematics	science of numbers and shapes
Matter	any substance which has mass and occupies space
Measurement	process of assigning numbers to observations or events
Meteorology	scientific study of the Earth's atmosphere, weather and climate
Metre rule	instrument used for measuring length up to 100 cm
Micrometre screw gauge	instrument used to measure small diameters with high precision
Motion	continuous movement involving a change in position relative to a reference point
Parallax error	error occurring when taking readings from a wrong eye position
Pascal's principle	external pressure applied to an enclosed liquid is transmitted equally throughout
Period	time taken by a pendulum to complete one full oscillation

[END_PAGE]

[PDF_PAGE 223]
[PRINTED_PAGE 217]

[DEFINITION_LIST]
Siphon	bent tube used for transferring liquid from one container to another using atmospheric pressure
Time	limited period during which an event occurred
Triple beam balance	scale used to measure the mass of objects in grams using three beams
Universe	all matter and energy that exists in space
Vernier calliper	instrument used to measure small lengths accurately
Viscous force	pull that resists the flow of a liquid
Work	done when a force acts on a body and moves it in the direction of the force
Zero error	condition when a measuring instrument registers a reading when the measured quantity is zero

[END_PAGE]
